Generation, repairs, and comparability

Every model receives the same unchanged user system prompt and saved dataset text, never MCQs, gold answers, existing summaries or KUs. Settings: temperature 0.2, top-p 0.95, thinking disabled, 16,000 output tokens, a 32,768-token context cap. Inputs and repairs are tokenized before inference; source text is never truncated. Context availability is the dataset text, not independently verified publisher PDFs. The two initial diagnostic papers had no bibliography, so absent citation fields must be empty rather than fabricated.

JSON-object constrained decoding prevents code fences; a conservative parser also recognizes a single enclosing fence and records its removal. Invalid fields are repaired by the same model using a separate, saved evidence-repair prompt and the original text. The primary stage has up to six calls; repair responses can modify only requested fields and have a 4,096-token budget. A second bounded stage (up to six calls) proposes real source fragments through lexical retrieval and asks the same model to choose supporting candidate indices or explicitly drop unsupported entries. Retrieval is not fuzzy validation: every selected quote is an actual source span, with candidates, offsets, selections and removals logged. Calls process at most eight quote tasks without truncating the source. Schema errors still get field-only patches. A failed cohort pass is resumed once; unresolved failures stop the queue, never silently dropping papers. Changing field order is logged. Every retained quote must match an actual source span and have at most five words. PDF whitespace/ligature alignment is logged, but mathematical substitutions are not accepted. Quote presence does not prove semantic entailment, and unsupported-entry removals can shorten the representation.

The fixed student remains Qwen/Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, BF16, vLLM 0.27.1, temperature 0.5, top-p 0.95, maximum 100 output tokens, frequency/presence penalties 1.05, historical ASCII sanitizer and semicolon parser. Five total format attempts; final invalid answers count as wrong. Frozen original/no-context/existing-summary/KU controls are reused only when question and input hashes match. The student receives substantive generated narrative, not evidence quotes or citation rankings. All model results use identical source IDs, questions and answer permutations.

27B uses mixed-INT4 weights on two RTX 3090s with vLLM and eight concurrent requests. Both requested 9B GGUFs use Q8_0 weights with llama.cpp on one RTX 3090 and four concurrent slots; KV cache is Q8_0. GGUF revisions and SHA-256 hashes are pinned in the manifest. Runtime, quantization, and batching differ; this is an end-to-end representation comparison, not an isolated model-weight ablation. Word targets in the prompt are not guaranteed; observed narrative lengths and repair rates are disclosed. Timing includes generation and repair attempts, excludes server startup, and allocated GPU-hours are not measured GH200/Jupiter timings.

Uncertainty uses 10,000 paired document-cluster bootstrap samples, seed 250219413, keeping each paper’s ten questions together. Differences within those intervals are not established improvements. This 97-paper convenience cohort and Luna-authored questions differ from the previous Physics/Medical benchmark.

Resume from the repository root: python experiments/scientific_summaries/launch_summary_comparison.py. The detached queue survives terminal closure, rejects duplicate queues, and supports explicit identity-checked adoption of an orphaned Qwen server; see the run instructions. Each model has separate summaries, source-only prompt snapshots, raw responses, token usage, student attempts and audited results under summary_runs/. Dataset: laion/Scientific-Summaries, declared CC-BY-4.0; source metadata remain saved. Model cards: Ornith, Qwen3.5.

All questions, source evidence, and per-model predictions

Multi-spectral Observations of Lunar Occultations: I. Resolving The Dust Shell Around AFGL 5440

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Why did the authors fit a lunar shadow velocity in addition to source size when modeling occultation fringes?
  • A: The uncertain lunar-limb shape can shift predicted shadow speed by tens of percent
  • B: The star's proper motion was comparable to the detector readout interval
  • C: The stellar pulsation changed the lunar angular diameter
  • D: The NaCl prism caused a time-dependent wavelength drift

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

Limb irregularities make the predicted fringe speed uncertain.

uncertainties in the shape of the lunar limb
Q2: Which limitation most directly prevented reliable detection of 10–30% departures from a uniform-disk photosphere in the comparison stars?
  • A: The Moon's phase was too close to new
  • B: Data/modeling accuracy was only about ±2 mas
  • C: No signal existed below 2.5 μm
  • D: The detector integrated for several seconds per frame

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

Their angular-size accuracy was inadequate for sub-milliarcsecond departures.

accuracies on the order of ±2 mas.
Q3: At what cadence was the detector read during AFGL 5440's lunar occultation, and what consequence did this have for the fringes?
  • A: Every 80 ms, leaving the fringes effectively instantaneous
  • B: Every 8 s, requiring interpolation across many missing cycles
  • C: Every 8 ms, so the Fresnel fringe pattern was time-averaged over each 8-ms integration
  • D: Every 1 ms, so no temporal averaging occurred

Gold: C · No context: B · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The detector integrated essentially the full 8-ms frame, averaging the fringe pattern over that interval (and the telescope aperture).

The detector is read-out every 8 msec, with photon integration occuring over essentially the full 8 msec time. Therefore, the Fresnel occultation fringe pattern is averaged over this 8 msec time (as well as over the 2.7-m telescope aperture).
Q4: What observation argued against representing AFGL 5440 with one uniform-disk size over the full band?
  • A: A disk fit only 1–2 μm and failed entirely above 2 μm
  • B: The fringe amplitude increased monotonically with wavelength
  • C: The 11-mas disk fit comparison stars but not AFGL 5440
  • D: An 11-mas disk gave fringes too sharp at long wavelengths and too broadened at short wavelengths

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The test disk was passable only in the mid-band and mismatched opposite wavelength ends.

To demonstrate the poor ﬁt with a single size uniform disk, we show in Figures 5 and 6 the results of trying to ﬁt the data with one example uniform disk, 11 mas. As can be seen in the plots of χ2 as well as the observed versus model light curves, the 11 mas disk provides a passable ﬁt in the mid-range of wavelengths, 2.5 – 3.2µm, but produces fringes that are too sharp at the longer wavelengths and too broadened at the shortest wavelengths.
Q5: Which inner-shell temperature constraint emerged from combined occultation and SED modeling, and what set its lower bound?
  • A: 950 ± 50 K; below 900 K there was too little hot dust to blur long-wavelength fringes
  • B: 750 ± 50 K; cooler dust overproduced the 60-μm flux
  • C: 1200 ± 100 K; hotter dust could not reproduce the 11-μm SiC feature
  • D: 500 ± 50 K; colder dust was incompatible with stellar diameter

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

Models below 900 K lacked enough warm dust for the observed near-IR occultation fringes.

First, the dust temperature at the inner radius of the dust shell was of order 950K ± 50K. Models with a maximum dust temperature below 900 K did not have enough hot dust to ﬁt the occultation fringes at the longer wavelengths, while models with hotter inner edges had even more diﬃculty than the best-ﬁt model in reproducing the energy distribu- tion longward of 10µm.
Q6: How did acceptable radial density laws behave across the near/mid-IR and far-IR constraints?
  • A: An r⁻²·² law uniquely fit fringes and solved the far-IR excess
  • B: An r⁻²-like law fit occultation and SED broadly, but comparable graphite-free models failed at 60 μm
  • C: An r⁻¹ law fit occultation, while r⁻² fit only 60 μm
  • D: A constant-density shell fit 3–60 μm but failed below 4 μm

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

Favored profiles were close to r⁻²; the 60-μm mismatch remained absent graphite or altered outer-shell history.

Secondly, the radial density gradient of models with a reasonable ﬁt was close to r−2 (or to DUSTY’s calculation of the gradient appropriate for a radiatively driven wind which approximates an r−2 distribution for large radii). Models with a density gradient of r−1.8 came closer to producing the IRAS 60µm ﬂux, but did not ﬁt the shape of the 5 – 20µm energy distribution well. Models with a density gradient of r−2.2 can ﬁt the energy distribution out to 20µm and also provide a good ﬁt to the occultation results, but have substantially worse ﬁts to the IRAS 60µm ﬂux than our best ﬁt models.
Q7: What tradeoff resulted from changing the density gradient from r⁻² to r⁻¹·⁸?
  • A: It improved occultation fit but erased the 11-μm SiC feature
  • B: It fixed the 5–20-μm SED while substantially worsening 60 μm
  • C: It moved toward the IRAS 60-μm flux but worsened the 5–20-μm SED shape
  • D: It forced inner temperature below 900 K and optical depth to zero

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The shallower law increased longer-wavelength emission at the cost of the mid-IR fit.

Models with a density gradient of r−1.8 came closer to producing the IRAS 60µm ﬂux, but did not ﬁt the shape of the 5 – 20µm energy distribution well.
Q8: Why did the best-fitting SED model include substantial graphite despite contrary evidence from comparable carbon stars?
  • A: It lowered dust temperature to 750 K
  • B: It prevented 3–4 μm emission from blurring occultation fringes
  • C: It alone created the 11-μm SiC feature
  • D: Its relatively flat 10–50 μm emissivity helped match the IRAS 60-μm flux

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Amorphous carbon plus SiC alone underpredicted 60 μm by at least a factor of three.

relatively ﬂat emissivity vs. wavelength dependence between 10 and 50µm
Q9: What alternative explanation did the authors offer for excess 60-μm emission if graphite is rejected?
  • A: Higher mass loss several hundred to a few thousand years ago left more cool dust at large radii
  • B: A recent increase placed extra hot dust inside the sublimation radius
  • C: A faster present wind enlarged the inner shell in occultation
  • D: A shorter distance shifted 11-μm emission into the 60-μm band

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

60-μm-emitting material lies far out and traces mass loss hundreds to thousands of years earlier.

If the mass-loss rate were greater in the past
Q10: Which pair best represents reported recent mass-loss estimates and their comparison?
  • A: Dust 3.1×10⁻⁵ versus gas 6.5×10⁻⁸ M☉/yr
  • B: Dust 6.5×10⁻⁸ M☉/yr here versus 5.1×10⁻⁸ previously; gas about 3.1×10⁻⁵ M☉/yr
  • C: Dust 5×10⁻⁶ versus gas 6.5×10⁻⁸ M☉/yr, both from occultation
  • D: Dust 10⁻⁹ versus gas 10⁻⁶ M☉/yr with exact agreement

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The modeled dust rate slightly exceeded the prior estimate, while gas loss was hundreds-fold larger.

The dust mass-loss rate implied by our derived optical depth and radial density dependence is ˙M dust = 6.5 × 10−8 M⊙/yr for the optical constants of amorphous carbon used by DUSTY (Ivezi´c, Nenkova & Elitzur 1999). Groenewegen et al. (2002) computed dust and gas mass-loss rates individually for AFGL 5440 on the basis of the IRAS 60µm ﬂux for the dust, and millimeter CO observations for the gas. They derived a gas mass-loss rate of 3.1×10−5 M⊙/yr and dust mass-loss rate of 5.1×10−8 M⊙/yr implying a gas-to-dust mass ratio of 600. Interestingly, their dust mass-loss rate is slightly lower than the value we have derived in spite of their using the 60µm IRAS ﬂux for normalization.

Gluon Radiation of an Expanding Color Skyrmion in the Quark-Gluon Plasma

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Under the high-temperature equation-of-state assumption ε=3p, what free-energy density does the paper derive as a function of invariant current length n?
  • A: F=βn^(2/3)/3, with β set by the temperature
  • B: F=βn^(4/3), with β equal to the effective gluon coupling
  • C: F=βn^(4/3)/4, with β a dimensionless integration constant
  • D: F=βn^(3/4)/4, with β fixed by the skyrmion radius

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper states ε=F and p=nF′−F; imposing ε=3p and integrating gives F=βn^(4/3)/4.

As a result, the free energy density can be obtained by integrating Eq. (3), F = β 4/3n4/3 (6) where β is a dimensionless constant of integration.
Q2: What does the paper's small parameter λ encode, and when is the expansion ansatz valid?
  • A: λ=τk, valid only for gluons below the inverse soliton size
  • B: λ=geff/β, valid when the effective coupling is perturbatively small
  • C: λ=R0/τ, valid when the soliton expands slowly compared with its size
  • D: λ=τ/R0, valid provided the expansion time τ is much shorter than R0

Gold: D · No context: B · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The authors define λ as the temporal-to-spatial scale ratio and state τ≪R0.

This condition enables us to deﬁne a small parameter
Q3: In the hedgehog ansatz, which sign convention corresponds to the skyrmion number +1?
  • A: The negative sign in cosφ=−(1−s²)/(1+s²)
  • B: The sign of sinφ, rather than the sign in cosφ, determines +1
  • C: Either sign, because the stereographic map has no topological charge
  • D: The positive sign in cosφ=(1−s²)/(1+s²)

Gold: A · No context: B · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The paper explicitly associates the negative cosφ sign with skyrmion number +1; it proceeds using the other sign.

The negative sign gives the skyrmion number +1 or a skyrmion
Q4: Which part of the current does the calculation retain when constructing its radiation spectrum?
  • A: The full current, including all higher-polarization modes
  • B: Only the spherically symmetric component in the third color component
  • C: All multipoles through the quadrupole, because higher modes dominate the UV
  • D: Only transverse components a=1,2, since the third component is topological

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The calculation neglects higher polarization modes and selects the spherical part, which appears only in component 3.

The spherically symmetric part in the current is contained only in the third component
Q5: What reasoning do the authors give for omitting gluon self-interactions and using a free-parton picture?
  • A: They establish that the effective coupling is much smaller than the partonic coupling from a perturbative calculation
  • B: They restrict the calculation to zero frequency, where gluon self-interactions are forbidden
  • C: They expect a hierarchy between partonic and effective couplings because hard-parton momentum transfer is much greater than transfer between a hard parton and the soliton
  • D: They show that the skyrmion current is color neutral, making all nonabelian vertices vanish

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper states the momentum-transfer ordering, expects a hierarchy between gYM and geff, and on that basis omits the self-interaction term; it does not specify which coupling is smaller.

Since the transfer momentum between hard partons is in high order to that between hard 3 parton and soliton, we expect a hierarchy between the partonic coupling gY M and the eﬀective coupling geff. Accordingly, gluon self-interaction in terms like F a µνF aµν can be omitted so we can work with a free parton picture.
Q6: What analytic object results from the spatial Fourier transform of the spherical current?
  • A: A Bessel function J4(Rk) multiplied by a Gaussian cutoff
  • B: A logarithmic kernel ln(1+Rk) times a cubic polynomial
  • C: A rational function with denominator (1+(Rk)²)^6
  • D: A time integral with phase e^(iωt−R(t)k) multiplied by Q4(R(t)k), where Q4(x)=5x²−5x³+x⁴

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The spatial integral is evaluated in closed form as the stated time integral and polynomial factor.

The spatial Fourier transformation can be completed analytically iM = B(k) Z dteiωt−R(t)kQ4(R(t)k) (16) where B(k) = π2A(k)/120 and Q4(x) = 5x2 −5x3 + x4.
Q7: After imposing the on-shell condition, how does the dimensionless frequency variable η relate to λ and κ?
  • A: η=λκ^(1/4), with κ=R0k
  • B: η=κ/λ, with κ=ωτ
  • C: η=λ/κ^(1/4), with κ=R0/ω
  • D: η=λκ^(3/4), with κ=k/R0

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The rescaled integral defines κ=R0k; setting ω=k gives η=λκ^(1/4).

η = λκ1/4 where κ is deﬁned to be R0k
Q8: What infrared power law distinguishes this finite-size soliton calculation from the cited infinite-medium result?
  • A: The energy spectrum scales as k^(−1), rather than k^(−1/2)
  • B: The number density scales as k^(−1/2), rather than k^(−1)
  • C: The number density scales as k^(−2), rather than k^(−1/2)
  • D: The number density approaches a constant, rather than k^(−1)

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The paper attributes the softer n∼1/√k behavior to finite medium size R0; the cited work gives 1/k.

the asymptotic of the number density in small k is ∼1/ √ k.
Q9: In the comparison with jet-quenching radiation, how do the authors define the characteristic medium-induced frequency ωC?
  • A: ωC= L/(2qhat)
  • B: ωC=1/(R0τ)
  • C: ωC= qhat L²/2
  • D: ωC=2qhat/L²

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper defines ωC as one half the quenching parameter times the square of the in-medium path length.

the medium induced gluon radiation is characterized by the frequency ωC = 1 2 ˆqL2 (24) where ˆq is the quenching parameter, estimated to be .04 ∼.16GeV 2/fm, and L is the in- medium path length of a hard parton
Q10: Which limitation/future direction do the authors identify for their present radiation calculation?
  • A: They cannot treat any time-dependent soliton and defer expansion effects
  • B: They omit the spherically symmetric mode and defer its calculation
  • C: They neglect color currents entirely and defer introducing an SU(2) field
  • D: They retain only the lowest polarization mode and defer higher polarizations

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The analysis explicitly restricts itself to the lowest mode; higher polarization effects are deferred.

the eﬀects of higher polarization will be considered elsewhere.

Are There Mach Cones in Heavy Ion Collisions? Three-Particle Correlations from STAR

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: In the STAR analysis, what event-mixing construction isolates the soft-soft three-particle background?
  • A: Keep the two associates from the same event but take the trigger from another event in the same centrality window
  • B: Mix all three particles from one event and subtract the measured v2
  • C: Fold two jet-like correlations and normalize at the away-side peak
  • D: Mix trigger and associates across events, adding trigger flow pairwise

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The associates remain from one event so their mutual correlations independent of the trigger are retained; the trigger comes from another event.

This background is obtained from mixed events
Q2: How was the non-Poisson multiplicity factor β determined in the background subtraction?
  • A: By scaling the mixed-event v4 term to 1.15 times the measured v2
  • B: By equating the background-subtracted jet-like pair yield with the square of the single-associate yield
  • C: By fitting the away-side peak width in pp and d+Au simultaneously
  • D: By requiring the subtracted triplet yield to equal the inclusive event multiplicity

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

β was set through a yield consistency relation between the three- and two-particle jet-like signals.

the number of associated pairs in the background subtracted jet-like three-particle correlation signal equals the square of the number of associated particles
Q3: Which centrality-dependent pattern distinguished the candidate conical signal from the away-side broadening?
  • A: A near-side peak shifted progressively away from zero in peripheral Au+Au
  • B: A diagonal peak at π disappeared in the highest-statistics central bin
  • C: An off-diagonal structure near π ± 1.45 radians grew with centrality
  • D: The pp away-side peak became narrower than the d+Au peak

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper reports a growing off-diagonal structure in more central Au+Au collisions, prominent in top-12% ZDC data.

the more central Au+Au collisions display an oﬀ-diagonal structure, at about π ± 1.45 radians
Q4: What did the measured associated-pT dependence of the off-diagonal angle imply for the two proposed emission mechanisms?
  • A: A linear increase favored deﬂected jets over conical emission
  • B: No angle could be extracted because the signal vanished above 1 GeV/c
  • C: A sharply decreasing angle supported Cherenkov radiation over hydrodynamics
  • D: A constant angle was observed, consistent with Mach-cone emission rather than the cited simple Cherenkov prediction

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The analysis found no strong pT dependence; the cited simple Cherenkov model predicts a sharply decreasing angle with increasing associated pT.

The angle is consistent with constant as a function of associated particle pT .
Q5: What normalization prescription was used for the two-particle background in the default analysis?
  • A: Normalize the mixed-event background to the signal in 0.8 < |Δφ| < 1.2 (ZYA1)
  • B: Normalize at the near-side peak over |Δφ| < 0.2
  • C: Normalize the background in 0.6 < |Δφ| < 1.4 from the outset
  • D: Normalize to the integral over the full azimuthal range

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The default scaling α used zero yield at one radian; widening that window was only a systematic check.

scale factor α) to the signal within
Q6: How did the authors estimate the systematic uncertainty from elliptic-flow subtraction?
  • A: They varied v2 from zero to the inclusive charged-particle yield
  • B: They used reaction-plane and four-particle-cumulant v2 values as upper and lower bounds
  • C: They replaced v2 with v4 and assigned the resulting difference
  • D: They compared only pp and d+Au since neither has flow

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The default used an average of the two v2 measurements, and their separate values bounded the systematic.

We use the reaction plane and 4-particle cumulant v2 as the upper and lower bounds
Q7: What was the measured location of the off-diagonal peaks from the double-Gaussian projection fit?
  • A: π/2 radians from zero
  • B: 1.30 radians from π
  • C: 1.45 radians from π
  • D: 1.00 radians from π

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The fitted off-diagonal locations were reported as 1.45 radians from π.

were found to be 1.45 radians from π.
Q8: Why could the on-diagonal/off-diagonal signal difference be interpreted as evidence about deflected jets?
  • A: The difference directly measures the v4 normalization uncertainty
  • B: The off-diagonal region is a pure flow background and the on-diagonal region is pure jet signal
  • C: Both regions contain only deflected-jet contributions, but have different acceptance
  • D: Conical emission is expected to be equal in both regions, while only the on-diagonal region can also contain deflected jets

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The paper states that the off-diagonal signal can contain conical emission only, whereas the on-diagonal can include deflected jets too.

where only conical emission contributes
Q9: Which sensitivity check addressed the α normalization window, and what was its reported implication?
  • A: The window was doubled to 0.6 < |Δφ| < 1.4, and the signal remained robust
  • B: The normalization was removed, and only the near-side peak survived
  • C: The window was narrowed to 0.9–1.1 rad, and the signal disappeared
  • D: The window was moved to π, producing a sign reversal

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

Doubling the normalization window was a systematic test and did not materially change the signal.

the size of the normalization window was doubled to 0.6 < |∆φ| < 1.4. The signal is robust
Q10: What was the principal caveat in the paper's interpretation of the central Au+Au off-diagonal peaks?
  • A: They conclusively measured a Mach cone and excluded all other mechanisms
  • B: They were consistent with conical emission and with Mach-cone expectations, but were not definitive proof of a Mach cone
  • C: They appeared equally in pp, d+Au, and Au+Au, preventing medium interpretation
  • D: Their angle varied strongly with pT, proving Cherenkov radiation

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The authors describe the structures as consistent with conical emission and say the pT result is consistent with Mach-cone emission, not as conclusive identification.

Oﬀ-diagonal peaks consistent with conical emission are present in central Au+Au collisions.

A schematic model of scattering in PT-symmetric Quantum Mechanics

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: In the discretized free regions, which constraint does requiring real lattice phase ϕ place on the original energy?
  • A: E must lie in (−4/h²,0)
  • B: E is unrestricted if M is finite
  • C: E must lie in (0,4/h²)
  • D: E must equal 2/h²

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The relation 2 cosϕ=2−h²E and real ϕ imply the stated finite-difference band.

reality of the new energy variable ϕ imposes the constraint
Q2: What does the paper identify as the actual innovation in its scattering construction?
  • A: Deriving a new definition of transmission unrelated to wave amplitudes
  • B: Using a nonlocal boundary condition instead of incoming and outgoing waves
  • C: Replacing the Schrödinger equation with a relativistic dispersion relation
  • D: Complexifying one potential-characterizing parameter within the discretized treatment

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Inertial Screening in Sedimentation

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: At what Reynolds number did the authors locate the onset of inertial corrections, and what length-scale comparison defines it?

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The onset is set by the inertial screening length matching the Stokes sedimentation length.

We ﬁnd that the onset of inertial corrections to Stokes sedimentation occurs when the inertial screening length l = a/Re becomes similar to the Stokes sedimentation length ξ0, at Rec = a/ξ0 ≈0.05. For Re > Rec, inertial screening signiﬁcantly reduces both the magnitude and spatial extent of the particle velocity ﬂuctuations.

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

They compared many concentrations 0.19 ≤φ ≤0.5, at both Re0 = 0.007 and Re0 = 0.3, and concluded that, contrary to expectations, the velocity ﬂuctuations were independent of Reynolds number for Re < 1. We ﬁrst note that they deﬁned Re0 ≡2aV0/η0 based upon the inﬁnite dilution values V0 and η0, not the values at the high concentrations studied. Using our deﬁnition Re = 2aV/η, we estimate that the Re0 = 0.3 samples range from 0.001 <∼Re <∼0.05. Our critical Reynolds number for the appearance of inertial inﬂuence, Rec = a/ξ0, can also be evaluated from their ﬁndings that ξ ∼11aφ−1/3, resulting in Rec ≈0.06. We therefore ﬁnd that Re <∼Rec for all of their samples, consistent with their ﬁndings of no inertial eﬀects..

  • B: Noise multiplies the pixel and follows a gamma distribution

  • D: Each pixel is replaced by one of two extrema with equal probability

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper describes additive Gaussian perturbations and gives mean and standard deviation as distribution parameters.

each pixel in the noisy image is the sum of the true pixel value and a random Gaussian distributed noise value.
Q2: Which property distinguishes the paper's salt-and-pepper noise description from its Gaussian model?
  • A: Every pixel receives an additive bell-distributed perturbation
  • B: The corruption is multiplicative and gamma distributed throughout the image

  • D: Corrupted pixels take only minimum or maximum intensity values while unaffected pixels remain unchanged

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The impulse noise has two extrema (0 and 255 in 8-bit images) and leaves other pixels untouched.

Unaffected pixels remain unchanged.

This type of noise occurs in almost all coherent imaging systems such as laser, acoustics and SAR

Brownian noise is a special case of 1/f noise. It is obtained by integrating white noise.

Nonlinearity in the Dark: Broadband Terahertz Generation with Extremely High Efficiency

Q1: What two simultaneous design features let the proposed metasurface suppress pump radiation yet retain nonlinear THz emission?

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The paired SRRs are arranged for destructive interference of linear electric/magnetic moments, while their second-order responses have the same sign and radiate constructively.

the nonlinear radiation of both SRRs adds constructively in the far ﬁeld. The linear radiation, how- ever, is opposite in phase and cancels in the far ﬁeld

we select the second quantized mode on the lowest branch of dispersion for the dielectric slab, that is, TE2,0 mode

Three-dimensional topological valley photonics

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What specific geometric change converts the nodal-chain phase of the model crystal into the reported complete-gap phase?
  • A: Increasing only the next-nearest rods removes time-reversal symmetry and gaps the valleys
  • B: Removing all nearest-neighbor rods converts the nodal lines to isolated Weyl points
  • C: Breaking inversion by making the two sphere diameters unequal gaps the nodal-line degeneracies
  • D: Applying a magnetic field separates the six valleys without changing inversion symmetry

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

At equal diameters inversion is retained and nodal lines occur; unequal diameters break inversion and gap them.

When ∆D = 0 (∆D ≡ |D1 − D2|), the inversion symmetry is preserved, and the photonic crystal hosts nodal lines along Wα± -X (α = x, y, z), forming intersecting nodal chains. The momentum of the six valleys are (±π/a, 2π/a, 0), (0, ±π/a, 2π/a), and (2π/a, 0, ±π/a) for Wx±, Wy±, and Wz±, respectively (Fig. 1(c)). When ∆D ≠ 0, however, the inversion symmetry is broken, and the nodal line degeneracies are gapped out to form a complete photonic bandgap.
Q2: What role do the 1.2-mm next-nearest-neighbor PEC rods play in the crystal design?
  • A: They create the four-source phase winding needed to excite Wy valleys
  • B: They supply the inversion breaking by connecting only the larger spheres
  • C: They tune the external boundary condition to θV = π/2
  • D: They suppress high-order modes, separating the topological bands from trivial bands

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The paper adds the thinner rods to clean up the band structure rather than to impose surface termination or source chirality.

Each nearest sphere is connected by PEC rods with diameter B1 = 2.4 mm. Additionally, we connect the next-nearest spheres by PEC rods with diameter B2 = 1.2 mm to suppress high-order modes, so that we can obtain a clean bandstructure where the topological bands are separated from the trivial bands [21].
Q3: How is the valley-pair distinction encoded in the local effective Hamiltonian?
  • A: The valley sign multiplies the two transverse momentum terms, while the inversion-breaking mass is m=(ω1−ω2)/2
  • B: Each pair is described by a scalar Dirac model with no momentum-dependent Pauli terms
  • C: The mass is set by the difference in rod diameters and is independent of inversion
  • D: The valley sign multiplies only the scalar mass, which equals the lattice constant

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The k·p model assigns valley-dependent signs to the two momentum terms and identifies the frequency splitting as the mass.

where (α, β, γ) = (x, y, z), (y, z, x), or (z, x, y), q = (qx, qy, qz) represents the momentum offset from each valley, vD is the group velocity, σx,y,z are Pauli matrices, and m = (ω1 − ω2)/2 is the mass term induced by breaking the inversion symmetry, with ω1,2 the angular frequencies of the two bands at the valleys [21].
Q4: In the Wy experiment at 13.8 GHz, which source phase winding selects Wy− and what direction is measured?
  • A: Equal phases select Wy− and outcouple at 0°
  • B: (0, π/2, π, 3π/2) selects Wy− and outcouples at −32.9°
  • C: (0, −π/2, −π, −3π/2) selects Wy− and outcouples at +32.9°
  • D: (0, π, 0, π) selects Wy− and outcouples at −90°

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The clockwise winding is assigned to Wy−, whose measured direction is the negative angle; the opposite winding selects Wy+.

Taking valley states at Wy± as an example, the relative phase delay of each source is set to (0, π/2, π, 3π/2) for Wy− and (0, −π/2, −π, −3π/2) for Wy+, to form a clockwise or counterclockwise phase winding in the x-z plane (see Fig. 2(a)). The excited valley states will outcouple to the free space radiation and, thus, the momentum of the excited valley state can be judged from the outcoupling direction (see Figs. 2(b) and (c)). As shown in Fig. 2(d), the measured outcoupling fields at 13.8 GHz exhibit different directions: for the clockwise (counterclockwise) phase winding, the direction is −32.9° (+32.9°), corresponding to Wy− (Wy+) , while for the case without phase winding, the direction is 0°, corresponding to X, since the states at X coexist with valley states at the same frequency [22].
Q5: What vectorial Chern assignments do the authors calculate for the three valley pairs?
  • A: Wx±:(0,±1/2,0), Wy±:(0,0,±1/2), Wz±:(±1/2,0,0)
  • B: Every valley carries the same scalar value +1/2 in all three directions
  • C: Wx±:(±1/2,0,0), Wy±:(0,±1/2,0), Wz±:(0,0,±1/2)
  • D: Each pair has zero Chern vector because time reversal forces all components to vanish

Gold: C · No context: D · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The nonzero component is aligned with the distinct directional valley pair, and its sign changes between ± valleys.

We calculate the valley Chern vector (VCV) [Cv = (Cvx, Cvy, Cvz)], which is (±1/2, 0, 0), (0, ±1/2, 0), and (0, 0, ±1/2) for Wx±, Wy±, and Wz± valleys, respectively [21].
Q6: What boundary tuning did the authors use to realize the chiral surface-state boundary condition on the (001) surface?
  • A: They created an opposite-valley domain wall and removed the plate
  • B: They chose θV=0 and used h=4 mm and D3=6.25 mm
  • C: They set h=a/2 and D3 equal to the larger bulk sphere diameter
  • D: They chose θV=π/2 and used a metallic plate with h=6.25 mm and D3=4 mm

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The derivation requires θV=π/2; the experimental termination was obtained by tuning the plate gap and outer-sphere diameter to the stated values.

We find that when h = 6.25 mm and D3 = 4 mm, the boundary condition can be satisfied [21].
Q7: Which observation did the authors use as experimental evidence for surface states in the bulk gap?
  • A: Surface transmission remained high while bulk transmission was substantially lower
  • B: The surface mode appeared only outside the bulk gap and had a flat dispersion
  • C: Both surface and bulk transmission vanished across the complete gap
  • D: The bulk transmission exceeded surface transmission at every gap frequency

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

High transmission confined to the surface together with lower bulk transmission is the reported signature of the gap-spanning surface channel.

The measured surface transmission, depicted in Fig. 3(e), maintains high within the complete photonic bandgap, while the bulk transmission is substantially lower, which verifies the existence of the surface states. We also map out the surface field distribution (Fig. 3(f)) and obtain its Fourier-transformed spectrum along L4- Wx−-L1 (Fig. 3(g)). We can indeed observe a nearly-linear surface dispersion in the bandgap.
Q8: What physical feature explains the reported diffractionless propagation of the surface state?
  • A: The measured state repeatedly scatters between opposite valleys
  • B: Its flat isofrequency contour preserves group velocity as transverse wavevector varies
  • C: A quadratic dispersion makes the group velocity vanish at all transverse momenta
  • D: A large density of bulk states traps the wave at the surface

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The paper attributes canalization to flat contours and notes that the dispersion is independent of the wavevector transverse to propagation.

Note that the chiral surface states exhibit canalization behaviour; that is, the surface states propagate diffractionlessly along the +y direction, corresponding to the Wx− valley. This canalization phenomenon is due to the flat isofrequancy contour of the surface states, which has been confirmed both experimentally and numerically (Fig. 3(h)) [25–27]. The canalization has previously been explored at the topological transition point between open hyperbolic and closed elliptical isofrequency contours, as in the twisted van der Waals bilayers [26]. From the equation of the surface states aforementioned, one can see that the surface dispersion is independent of the wavevector vertical to the propagation direction (denoted as kx (ky)), indicating that the group velocity of surface states preserves for different ky (kx) around each valley, which leads to the flat isofrequency contours.
Q9: For a Wx− state crossing from the (001) to (010) face, what path and robustness did the experiment report?
  • A: It travels −y then +z but is reflected at the corner by intervalley scattering
  • B: It travels +z then +y and requires a bulk mode to cross the bend
  • C: It travels +y then −z and passes the sharp corner with high transmission without intervalley scattering
  • D: It remains on the (001) face because its valley has no projection on (010)

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The measured surface channel follows the valley-locked direction around the corner while maintaining high transmission.

The excited surface states propagate along the +y direction, corresponding to Wx− valley. Owing to the valley conservation, the surface states smoothly pass through the sharp corner with high transmission (see Fig. 4(c)) and propagate along the −z direction. We also perform Fourier transform on the field distributions on the (001) and (010) surfaces, respectively.
Q10: What limitation of earlier 3D topological photonic insulators motivates the all-surface geometry claimed here?
  • A: They had no time-reversal symmetry and therefore no valleys
  • B: Their states were confined to domain walls and could not be measured
  • C: They required optical rather than microwave frequencies
  • D: Their topological surface states were restricted to certain faces, whereas this valley crystal can host them on every sample surface

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors contrast face-restricted states of earlier systems with their shape supporting chiral valley states on all three orthogonal surface orientations.

Unlike the existing 3D topological photonic insulators [29–34] in which the topological surface states can only exist on certain surfaces, the 3D VPCs with certain shapes can support the chiral valley surface states on all sample surfaces. Obviously, our fabricated sample can support the chiral valley surface states on all (100), (010), and (001) surfaces, with the propagation direction locked to each valley.

Dense-TSNet: Dense Connected Two-Stage Structure for Ultra-Lightweight Speech Enhancement

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What does Dense-TS change in the classic Two-Stage network to address narrow-model optimization?
  • A: It replaces the dilated convolutions in the Dilated Dense Block with Two-Stage modules and densely connects them
  • B: It predicts phase only and bypasses time-frequency feature extraction
  • C: It removes all dense connections and uses a wider Transformer encoder
  • D: It replaces the Two-Stage modules with a single dilated convolutional layer

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The architecture is made of densely connected Two-Stage modules instead of the classic dilated block followed by serial TS modules.

To address these issues, we propose the Dense-TS structure, shown in Fig. 1(b). This structure replaces dilated convolutions in Dilated Dense Block with Two-Stage modules, resulting in a network composed entirely of densely connected Two-Stage modules.
Q2: How are temporal and frequency dependencies handled within one classic Two-Stage feature extraction module?
  • A: The map is pooled to B×T×F and processed only along frequency
  • B: The map is reshaped first to BF×T×C and then BT×F×C before restoring the original shape
  • C: The map is split into real and imaginary parts and processed by separate decoders
  • D: The map is flattened to BTF×C and processed as an unordered sequence

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The two reshapes let serial modules model temporal and frequency dependencies separately.

Given a spectral feature map D ∈RB×T ×F ×C, D is reshaped to DT ∈RBF ×T ×C for temporal dependencies and then reshaped to DF ∈RBT ×F ×C for frequency dependencies, with the final output Do reshaped to the input size.
Q3: Which combination defines the output of a Dense-TS block, beyond concatenating the intermediate layer features?
  • A: It concatenates the input with only the first TS-MVGB output
  • B: It multiplies the deepest feature by 0.2 and applies a sigmoid
  • C: It adds the input x to 0.2 times the final dense skip representation
  • D: It averages all skips equally and removes the original input

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Equation (4) uses a scaled final skip tensor plus the original block input, adding an extra residual route.

output = 0.2 ∗skipdepth−1 + x
Q4: Which view and signal features are chiefly associated with LKE versus LSG in MVGB?
  • A: LKE and LSG are both recurrent modules that model only temporal context
  • B: LKE is channel attention for high-frequency detail; LSG is the global long-range branch
  • C: LKE estimates phase directly; LSG reconstructs the clean waveform after ISTFT
  • D: LKE supplies a large-receptive-field global view for long-range low-frequency features; LSG models local information and high-frequency detail

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors distinguish LKE's global context from LSG's local spatial mask modeling.

The large kernel depthwise convo- lutions provide a large receptive field to capture long-range low-frequency features, while Simple Gate and HardSwish are designed with lightweight considerations. The input x is normalized and then divided into two branches. Channel Attention (CA) The first branch is Channel At- tention (CA) [15], which reallocates weights to each channel, allowing the module to focus more on important channels while suppressing less significant ones, resulting in a more precise feature representation [19]. Learnable-Sigmoid Gate (LSG) The LSG shown in Fig. 2 is responsible for establishing local spatial perspectives, consists of depth-wise convolution, point-wise convolution, and Learnable-Sigmoid activation function. This branch is inspired by the Learnable-Sigmoid activation function used in MP-SENet [5] for generating masks. Its flexibility makes it crucial for generating accurate masks. This branch focuses on modeling local information within the feature map, enhancing high-frequency details and spatial invariance in the spectrum.
Q5: What does the paper's Consistency Magnitude Loss compare?
  • A: The clean magnitude spectrum and the magnitude spectrum obtained by applying STFT after ISTFT to the network output
  • B: The discriminator score for clean speech against a constant zero target
  • C: The noisy and clean complex spectra using a learned PESQ discriminator
  • D: The estimated phase with the target waveform after time-domain filtering

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The authors compute a minimum distance in magnitude space after synthesizing and reanalyzing the enhanced speech.

We directly compute the minimum distance between the magnitude spectrum of the twice-STFTed speech and the clean speech magnitude spectrum, as shown in Fig. 3. This loss LMagConsis.is defined as Consistency Magnitude Loss:
Q6: Why do the authors avoid making Metric Loss part of the final Dense-TS objective despite PESQ gains in some trials?
  • A: Metric Loss reduces parameter count below the hardware-supported minimum
  • B: Large metric-loss weighting increases spectral discrepancies and can introduce pseudohallucination that harms perceived quality
  • C: Metric Loss improves only phase error while the model predicts phase directly
  • D: Metric Loss is non-differentiable even after use of a discriminator

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The reported P=200 condition produced pseudohallucination and the P=400 condition failed, so the final weight for that term is zero.

Although the PESQ score increases with P, the distances between amplitude, phase, and the complex spectrum also increase. Moreover, when P is too large (e.g., P = 400), training fails. For instance, as shown in Fig. 5, a pseudohallucination distortion is introduced at P = 200, affecting the perceptual quality. Given these observations, we do not employ LMetric in Dense-TS.

The training process was conducted with a batch size of 2, using the AdamW optimizer [23], and was carried out for up to 1,000,000 steps.

According to the PESQ curves, while the classic Two-Stage structure converges faster in the early stages, the Dense-TS framework can achieve a better performance in the later stages. Recurrent Interpolants for Probabilistic Time Series Prediction

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What scalability contrast motivates combining recurrent networks with a generative model for time-series prediction?
  • A: Diffusion models require no context window, while recurrent models model the full joint density
  • B: A recurrent model scales with window length only, while diffusion scales with feature count only
  • C: A recurrent model's size depends on feature count rather than window length, whereas a joint-window generative predictor scales with both
  • D: Both model sizes are independent of feature dimension, but only the RNN scales with sequence length

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper says conventional recurrent model size is proportional to feature count, while generative predictors model a window whose dimensionality includes both features and temporal extent.

the model size is only proportional to the number of features, but not the length of the time window
Q2: What data-distribution intuition leads the authors to initialize prediction from the current time point rather than Gaussian noise?
  • A: The current point has higher entropy than the future and is therefore easier to sample
  • B: The context window is always Gaussian after RNN encoding
  • C: Future values are independent of the current observation, making a Gaussian prior exact
  • D: Neighboring time points are strongly correlated, so the future distribution is close to the current point's distribution

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors reason that a nearby terminal distribution should ease transport learning, reduce inference complexity, and improve sample quality.

Time series data is typically continuous and neighboring time points exhibit strong correlations, indicating that the distribution of future time points is close to that of the cur- rent time point.
Q3: What pair of time-series values does the proposed conditional SI module couple during training?
  • A: Adjacent observations xt and xt+1, conditioned on the history xt−P:t−1
  • B: The first and last values of a sequence, without recurrent context
  • C: Two randomly selected future observations, conditioned only on feature dimension
  • D: The entire context window and an independent Gaussian vector, conditioned on the prediction horizon

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The model learns the conditional joint of consecutive points given prior observations; the RNN summarizes that history as conditioning information.

the generative model learns the joint distribution p(xt+1, xt|xt−P :t−1) of the pair (xt+1, xt) given the past observations xt−P :t−1.
Q4: Why does the model encode history with an RNN instead of directly interpolating from the whole context window to the next point?
  • A: The context window is missing during training and must be imputed first
  • B: The stochastic interpolant endpoints must have the same sample-space size, so the RNN compresses history into a conditioning prompt
  • C: The RNN is needed to make the endpoint distributions independent
  • D: The SI score function accepts categorical inputs only, so continuous context cannot be used

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The paper notes the different vector sizes of full context and next-value target; an RNN converts the history into a fixed conditioning representation while SI transports xt to xt+1.

As the sample spaces of ρ0 and ρ1 must be the same, the generative model can not directly map the whole context window
Q5: What sampling strategy addresses large loss contributions near the endpoints of the stochastic-interpolant time interval?
  • A: Use a Gaussian proposal centered at 0.5 without importance weights

  • D: Set interpolation time to 0 or 1 with equal probability

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Algorithm 1 samples s from the Beta proposal and weights each loss contribution by the inverse sampling density to stabilize endpoint integration.

si ∼Beta(0.1, 0.1).

Freeze-Float System for High-throughput Measurement of Ice Nucleation

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Why did the authors add a good heat conductor between parallel cuvettes instead of simply enlarging the original silicone-oil footprint?
  • A: Larger cuvettes could not contain enough aqueous droplets to reach statistical power
  • B: The initial freezer could not cool any sample below −0.5 °C/min
  • C: Low silicone-oil conductivity caused thermal lag across a larger footprint and several cuvettes had degree-scale differences
  • D: Parallel cuvettes made the freeze-float separation chemistry incompatible with HFE-7500

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The original 1-cm² cuvette held up to 50 droplets, but scaling its footprint or running many cuvettes together produced nonuniform temperatures.

1 cm 2 geometry cuvette system successfully accommodated up to 50  1 L droplets at once and separated the samples that have ice-nucleating activity from those do not. However, due to the low thermal conductivity of the silicone oil, it was not possible to increase the footprint of the container and accommodate a larger number of droplets without introducing a significant thermal lag. The throughput could be increased by setting up multiple cuvettes in parallel, but setting up more than 5-10 cuvettes in the same freezer system was difficult, and again, resulted in non-uniform results; for example, in setting up 10 cuvettes at the same time, we observed a difference of a few degrees' between cuvettes.
Q2: What was the statistical basis for concluding that more than 30 droplets suffice for a reliable freezing temperature estimate?
  • A: They used a binomial test with 30 droplets fixed by the 96-well plate geometry
  • B: They selected 30 because the empirical standard deviation became zero at that sample size
  • C: They used a Kolmogorov-Smirnov p-value threshold that requires 30 droplets per class
  • D: They used n≈(2k/CI)²s², with k=1.96 for 95% confidence, and concluded over 30 droplets suffice

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The sample-count estimate relates target confidence interval to the outcome standard deviation and the normal critical value.

Confidence interval of the freezing temperature of droplets was calculated via the formula: 𝑛 ≈ (2𝑘 𝐶𝐼 ⁄ ) 2 𝑠 2 𝑛 is the sample number, 𝑘 is the value for desired confidence level (1.96 for 95% confidence), 𝐶𝐼 is the confidence interval, and 𝑠 is the standard deviation of the outcome of interest. From the obtained value, we concluded that more than 30 droplets sample is sufficient to observe the reliable freezing temperature.
Q3: How did automated dispensing translate into the stated theoretical throughput in the 96-deep-well arrangement?
  • A: The system could put 40 uniform droplets into each of 96 wells at one droplet per 2 seconds, totaling roughly 3,800 in about 15 minutes
  • B: It produced 60 droplets in each 12-well plate in under one minute
  • C: It generated 1,920 droplets per well by using a multi-head dispenser
  • D: It generated 30 droplets in each of 48 vials at 40 droplets per second

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The authors give the per-well capacity, dispensing rate, total approximate count, and time for the Biomek protocol.

At a standard dispensing rate 1 droplet per 2 seconds, a pipetting arm successfully generated 40 uniform droplets in 96 wells (up to ~3800 droplets at once) in ~15 minutes. The rate can be further accelerated by using a multi-head dispenser.
Q4: What did the 60-droplet fluorescein assay establish about the robotic droplet volume distribution?
  • A: The system produced a bimodal distribution with 40% coalescence
  • B: The radius coefficient of variation was 9.2%, based on SD 2.86×10⁻⁵ m and mean 3.10×10⁻⁴ m
  • C: The droplets had a 36% temperature dispersion despite identical volume
  • D: The diameter coefficient of variation was 2.3%, indicating a 2.3 °C freezing spread

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The paper defines polydispersity as radius SD divided by mean radius and reports those values for the 60 droplets.

The polydispersity of the 60 droplets defined as standard deviation of the radii of the droplets (here 2.8610 -5 ) divided by the mean radius (here 3.1010 -4 m) was 9.2%, which indicated that the droplets generated by the robotic system have sufficiently uniform volume (Figure 1 D-E ).
Q5: What freezing-temperature uncertainty did the authors infer from that droplet-size variability?
  • A: At most ±2.3 °C, because that was measured for the robotic droplet batch
  • B: At most ±0.59 °C, based on Snomax freezing in the controlled freezer
  • C: At most ±0.09 °C, using the radius variation and the stated logarithmic freezing-temperature relation
  • D: At most ±1.6 °C, based on well-to-well standard deviation

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

They map the radius standard deviation through y=ln(x)−26.8 and estimate a narrow expected nucleation-temperature spread.

Swanson and co-worker observed that the droplet diameter and homogeneous freezing temperature can be expressed as 𝑦 = ln(𝑥) -26.8 where 𝑦 is nucleation temperature and 𝑥 is droplet radius. 21 Based on the standard deviation of the radii of the droplets, we expected no more than 0.09 °C dispersion in the measurement of the nucleation temperature.
Q6: Which failure mechanism prevented straightforward transfer of the automated generation stage into the larger workflow?

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The conclusion notes that droplet stability during transfer was inadequate because static electricity from friction led to merging.

However, when the system needs to be transferred certain distance, for example from one laboratory to another, the droplet stability was not sufficient and static electricity caused by frictions caused coalescing. Due to this problem, it was not successful to combine the droplets generation system.
Q7: How did the controlled-rate freezer distinguish INA-containing Snomax droplets from INA-free water in the reported vial experiment?

The controlled cooling provided the separation between inoculated and INA-free droplet populations.

Similar to the previous observations, the droplets with INA froze at -7.1 0.59 °C and droplets without INA did not freeze until the temperature reached to -12.3 °C in the Asymptote freezer (Figure 4 ). This observation suggested that the controlled cooling rate provided better detection of INA compared to the freeze-float selection in a commodity freezer system (mean temperature -7.5 0.97 °C).
Q8: What did the authors find when comparing well-to-well variability in the 96-well generic freezer versus the Asymptote system?

  • B: Asymptote reduced dispersion from SD 1.6 to 0.73 and yielded p=0.83 versus p=0.31

We observed well to well variability and interwell dispersion in the freezing time in the 96 deep well system (p = 0.31, standard deviation 1.6), whereas the distribution of freezing of droplets in Asymptote freezer (Figure 5 ) showed less difference of well to well freezing temperature (p = 0.83, standard deviation 0.73) and overall 36% lower dispersion (Figure 6 ).
Q9: Why did linear cooling improve the comparison between wells relative to holding a generic freezer at a constant temperature, yet remain imperfect?
  • A: It reduced the p-value from 0.31 to 4.4×10⁻⁷ by accelerating freezing
  • B: It lowered the mean freezing time by 36 minutes but increased temperature gradients
  • C: The p-value improved from 4.4×10⁻⁷ to 0.31, but freezing times still differed and the means were 47.4 versus 50.6 minutes
  • D: It made all wells freeze simultaneously at p=0.83 and eliminated timing differences

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Linear cooling reduced but did not eliminate heterogeneity; the authors explicitly note significantly longer time spread and distinct mean times.

The freezing temperature in cells in the same deep well plate showed p-value of 4.410 -7 at constant temperature -28 °C generic freezer (Figure 3 ). The thermal gradient was decreased (p = 0.31) with linear cooling (-0.25 °C/min) compared to the constant temperature cooling, however it significantly increased the range of freezing time, and mean freezing time between the cells were different (47.4 and 50.6 min).
Q10: What scale-up capacity and cooling design addressed the earlier cuvette throughput ceiling?
  • A: Sixty droplets in each of 12 vials, with no temperature control
  • B: Thirty droplets in each of 96 wells with silicone oil alone as the heat conductor

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors expanded vessel scale and inserted conductive hardware to improve vial-to-vial uniformity.

By expanding the size of the assay vessel from a 1 cm 2 footprint cuvette that contains up to 50 droplets to the 48 cryovials that can contain up to 40 droplets (40 droplets × 48 vials = 1920 droplets), the throughput can be increased by the factor of 38. The cooling setup employed the metal cooling plate and sample holder to achieve the homogeneous cooling through the vial to vial.

Tuning the Dielectric Response in a NC Material through Nanoparticle Morphology

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Why was MgO selected as the matrix for the Ag molecular-nanoparticle calculations?
  • A: Its cubic structure and isotropic polarization simplify calculations, with few polarizable d-electrons
  • B: Its low bandgap matches silver and creates metallic percolation
  • C: It has a higher dielectric constant than HfO2 and no ionic charges
  • D: Its anisotropic polarization reproduces the behavior of SiO2

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The paper emphasizes computational convenience from cubic symmetry, isotropy, and limited polarizable d states.

An inert material with a bulk dielectric constant of 8-9.8 and a bandgap of 7.8 eV, MgO is one of the most convenient crystalline oxides from a computational standpoint, due to its cubic crystal structure and isotropic polarization, (unlike SiO 2 which can present anisotropic polarization depending on its structure), and a low number of polarizable d-electrons (unlike other mid/high-κ materials, such as HfO 2 ).
Q2: How did the authors separate predominantly ionic and electronic contributions to the low-frequency response?
  • A: They applied a 0.001-a.u. field along x and measured current at Γ
  • B: They compared damped-dynamics polarization with fully relaxed electronic polarization after applying a 0.001-a.u. z-field
  • C: They compared two temperatures and assigned their difference to electronic response
  • D: They computed only the zero-field Born charges and inferred both contributions

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The damped state is described as mainly ionic, whereas the fully relaxed in-field state captures electronic polarization and Ag charge motion toward the interface.

In QE, this was achieved using static field (ε field ) of 0.001 a.u applied along the z-axis. The reference polarization is that of the cell without an applied field. The electronic and ionic polarization is calculated using damped dynamics ( P ε damped ) shows mainly ionic polarization. After fully relaxing the electronic structure in the field (P ε relax ) describes electronic polarization as the electrons are allowed to polarize and the Ag electron density is allowed to move into the Ag-MgO interface.
Q3: What finite-size and periodic-boundary caveat applies to the reported εr values?
  • A: They cannot be compared because calculations used open boundaries
  • B: They include all surface charges but omit the matrix volume
  • C: They are volume averages that omit real-material surface-charge and edge effects
  • D: They are true bulk constants independent of inclusion loading

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper cautions that the periodic simulated cell produces a volume average and does not represent surface and edge contributions of a real material.

The values calculated for ε r are not 'true' dielectric constants for a real material in that surface charge and edge effects are not taken into account as the material simulated is periodic and the value is a volume average.
Q4: Why do CPMD polarization trajectories sometimes require quantum-of-polarization corrections?
  • A: Ag atoms diffuse into neighboring cells, changing the stoichiometry
  • B: The ionic matrix melts and reverses the sign of every Born charge
  • C: The field is too large and causes dielectric breakdown in every trajectory
  • D: Electron density can cross a periodic box boundary, shifting reported polarization by a box-length quantum

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The polarization reference coordinate changes when particle-like electrons polarize across the periodic image boundary.

The explicit 'particle-like' electrons in CPMD will occasionally polarize into the neighboring image cell (or, more specifically, to the other side of the simulation box), shifting the charge distribution relative to the 'reference' coordinate system in which the polarization is calculated. The reported polarization then shifts (positive or negative) in value equal to nxboxlength.
Q5: What geometric extent of Ag-induced structural distortion was generally reported, and how was periodic-image interaction limited?
  • A: Distortion reached about 3 Å from the inclusion edge; two Mg–O layers surrounded it
  • B: Distortion was restricted to the Ag atoms; no matrix buffer was added
  • C: Distortion extended across the whole cell; one Mg–O layer was used
  • D: Distortion extended 30 Å; a vacuum gap replaced the MgO matrix

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The local strain was limited to nearby neighbors, and matrix layers were added to keep it from reaching periodic images.

The distortion, or 'stress', of the crystal structure only extended in general to the nearest neighbors of the inclusions (up to around 3 angstroms from the edge of the mNP). Sufficient MgO layers were added around the inclusion so that the NP-induced distortion did not extend to neighboring cells
Q6: Which morphology/orientation comparison shows why dielectric enhancement is not determined by inclusion length alone?
  • A: Rod224 and Disk332 are identical shapes and have opposite polarization
  • B: Rod224 and Disk332 have similar loading but different shapes, and Disk332 has a similar response with fewer atoms
  • C: Disk442 is smaller than Rod223 but gives less than half its response
  • D: Ag8 has no enhancement while every larger inclusion has exactly the same value

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The authors explicitly compare these differently shaped inclusions at similar loading to caution against a simple length/electron-count rule.

However, dielectric enhancement in general is significantly more complex than a simple correlation to the mNP length or the availability of polarizable electrons. This is evidenced by comparison of ε for Rod224 and Disk332: Both mNPs have similar loading but different shapes. Disk332 shows a similar value to Rod224 with fewer atoms.
Q7: What difference did the calculations report between 3D and 2D disk-like inclusions?
  • A: 2D disks enhanced polarization more than all 3D disks because electrons move freely along z
  • B: Only the 2D disks increased polarization, while 3D disks matched MgO
  • C: 3D disks 332, 432, and 442 had higher polarization than similarly sized rods, while 2D disks 421 and 441 reduced it below bulk in both directions
  • D: Both classes had identical response because shape did not affect electron motion

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The authors attribute the 2D decrease to confinement of inclusion electrons against motion in the z direction.

The 3dimensional mNPS of (332,432 and 442) all showed a higher polarization than similarly sized rods likely due to more moving electrons with greater space to move in the nanoparticle. The 2-Dimensional nanoparticles (421 and 441) show a decrease in polarization when compared to the bulk in both directions. Since the inclusion electrons are not free to move in the z-direction they do not contribute to the increased average polarization.
Q8: Where were the strongest enhanced polarization fields found for the 224 rod inclusion, and how did the equatorial region differ?
  • A: Both poles and equator showed equal depolarization
  • B: Enhancement appeared only at the equator; poles showed depolarization
  • C: The field was uniform throughout the matrix and absent at the interface
  • D: Enhancement appeared near the poles along the field; the equator showed depolarization

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

BEC maps indicate stronger response at the nanoparticle poles and lower effective ionic charge around the equator.

Enhancement of the polarization field is seen in the matrix in regions adjacent to the NP poles, in the direction of the applied field. In the equatorial region a depolarization arises (red/yellow regions in BEC plots) where the effective ionic charge is less than in the absence of the external field.
Q9: What did the authors identify as a key practical limitation when interpreting orientation-dependent dielectric response?
  • A: Perpendicular polarization can create screening/depolarization that lowers the average dielectric constant, so orientation and inclusion separation matter
  • B: Parallel alignment always produces a lower response than perpendicular alignment
  • C: Disk inclusions eliminate interface fields and behave as bulk silver
  • D: Loading percentage alone fixes the response and makes aggregation irrelevant

The paper notes destructive perpendicular response can dominate and calls for careful control of direction, loading, aggregation, and separation.

sometimes this perpendicular response in the matrix dominates the overall polarization and the dielectric constant cal-culated is lower than for pure matrix 1. close control on direction must be maintained, 2. %loading is not the only thing, aggregation and separation Is very important
Q10: How did multilayer disk inclusions compare with the MgO matrix at similar volume loading?
  • A: They enhanced it by 30% only when loading exceeded 50%
  • B: Disks 332, 432, and 442 produced more than 70% dielectric enhancement
  • C: They reduced the dielectric constant by more than 70%
  • D: They produced exactly the same permittivity as Ag8

Low density interior in supercooled aqueous nanodroplets expels ions to the subsurface

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What low-temperature structural change did the authors associate with the displacement of Na⁺ in droplets of at least 200 water molecules?
  • A: A liquid-like core with ions concentrated at the vapor interface
  • B: A crystalline surface layer surrounding an amorphous, high-density core
  • C: A tetrahedrally ordered low-density core surrounded by a denser, more disordered subsurface
  • D: A uniformly dense core surrounded by a low-density surface shell

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

At 200 K the larger droplets develop a low-density tetrahedral core and high-density subsurface; Na⁺ favors that more disordered subsurface rather than the core.

a lowdensity core with relatively good tetrahedral ordering 31 is surrounded by a high density subsurface.
Q2: Which comparison best captures the single-Na⁺ radial distribution at 300 K versus 200 K for droplets with N ≥ 200?
  • A: At both temperatures it peaks at the vapor interface, but the 200 K peak is narrower
  • B: At 300 K it peaks at the COM; at 200 K it becomes uniform throughout the droplet
  • C: At 300 K it follows the tetrahedral core; at 200 K it follows the surface number-density maximum
  • D: At 300 K it is nearly uniform in the interior; at 200 K its peak lies within 0.1 nm of the peak in Voronoi-based water density

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The simulations show an approximately constant interior ion density at high temperature, whereas at low temperature the ion peak tracks the subsurface peak in ρv(r).

Rather than being centered at r = 0, the peak of ρ N a (r) is located within 0.1 nm of the peak in ρ v (r) (for N ≥ 200).
Q3: How was the water-density profile ρv(r) constructed, and what physical distinction did the authors use it to make?
  • A: From Voronoi volumes around oxygen sites; it distinguishes fully surrounded interior/subsurface molecules from low-density surface molecules
  • B: From oxygen number density only; it identifies the surface as the region of maximum molecule count
  • C: From dipole moments in each shell; it identifies the core as the region of strongest orientational polarization
  • D: From hydrogen-bond counts per shell; it distinguishes liquid water from vapor by tetrahedral order alone

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The authors average density from oxygen-site Voronoi volumes, which become large at the surface; this allows them to identify surface molecules separately from fully surrounded ones.

Surface molecules can be identified as those located where ρ v (r) is small, and molecules in the subsurface as those located an intermolecular distance below the point at which ρ v (r) has decayed to near zero.
Q4: Which limitation did the authors identify for interpreting the ion distribution in the smallest, N=100 droplet at 200 K?
  • A: It contains too few ions for the one-ion distribution to be computed
  • B: It lacks a tetrahedrally organized core and has large relative shape fluctuations, with Na⁺ often one or two layers inward from the surface
  • C: It equilibrates too rapidly for the ion density to be measured and always places Na⁺ at the COM
  • D: Its core density is so high that the ion is excluded to the vapor phase

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The smallest droplet does not form the low-density tetrahedral core characteristic of larger supercooled droplets; shape fluctuations are relatively large and Na⁺ tends to reside near the surface.

The cluster of N = 100 does not show a tetrahedrally organized core. It is noted that this smaller cluster is subject to larger relative shape fluctuations than the larger droplets.
Q5: What equilibration check was used for the N=776, 200 K single-ion simulations initialized with Na⁺ at different locations?
  • A: They initiated all ions at the surface and judged equilibration by the number of evaporated waters
  • B: They compared Na⁺ trajectories in different force fields and stopped after 400 ps
  • C: They compared runs initialized at/near the COM and at the surface, finding radial ion densities converged after 400 ns
  • D: They compared runs at 200 K and 300 K and considered them equilibrated once the temperatures matched

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

To test equilibration, they started independent simulations with Na⁺ near the center or surface and used convergence of radial ion densities; for N=776 convergence occurred after 400 ns.

For example, for N = 776 after 400 ns, the ion densities as a function of radial distance from the droplet COM ρ ion (r) converge for the two simulations.
Q6: What simulation model and integration settings were used for the aqueous droplets?
  • A: TIP3P water, OPLS-AA Na⁺, Berendsen thermostat with 2 ps time constant, and 1 fs leap-frog steps
  • B: TIP4P/2005 water, CHARMM Na⁺, microcanonical dynamics, and 0.5 fs Brownian steps
  • C: SPC/E water, AMBER Na⁺, Langevin thermostat with 2 fs time constant, and 0.1 ps velocity-Verlet steps
