Gold: D · No context: A · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The methods specify TIP4P/2005 water, OPLS-AA sodium, Nosé–Hoover temperature control, and leap-frog integration at 2 fs.

The temperature is controlled by using the Nosé-Hoover thermostat with time constant 0.1 ps. The equations of motion are integrated with the leap-frog algorithm with a time step of 2 fs.
Q7: What does the paper's electrostatic-confinement model predict for a single ion, and under what conditions is this effect more pronounced?
  • A: A harmonic potential centered at the droplet COM, enhanced by low temperature, high charge, small radius, and intermediate dielectric constant
  • B: A harmonic potential centered at the droplet surface, enhanced by low charge and large radius
  • C: A constant potential independent of ion position, enhanced only when the dielectric constant approaches infinity
  • D: A repulsive potential centered at the surface, enhanced by high temperature, low charge, and large radius

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The model assigns a COM-centered harmonic potential and predicts greater electrostatic confinement at low temperature, high charge, small radius, and intermediate dielectric constant.

Analyzing Eqs. ( 2) and (4) we conclude that the effect will be more pronounced at low temperature, high charge, small radius and intermediate values of the dielectric constant.
Q8: For five Na⁺ ions in the 776-water droplet at 300 K, how did simulation profiles compare with the paper's nonlinear Poisson–Boltzmann reference?
  • A: The fitted decay length was 0.8 nm, matching the 1.2 nm prediction exactly and validating a rigid-sphere model quantitatively
  • B: The fitted decay length was about 1.2 nm, while using water dielectric constant 75 gave about 0.8 nm; the authors treated the comparison qualitatively because shape fluctuations matter
  • C: The model predicted 1.3 nm for 776 waters and simulations gave 0.42 nm, which the authors attributed to ion pairing
  • D: The simulation showed no radial decay, whereas the model predicted a 1.2 nm decay length independent of droplet size

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

For five ions in 776 waters at 300 K the fitted exponential has λPB≈1.2 nm, compared with ≈0.8 nm from ε=75; significant shape fluctuations led the authors to caution against quantitative interpretation.

For 776 H 2 O molecules -5 Na + ions at T = 300 K the distribution decays (toward the droplet's COM) as an exponential function fitted by 0.42 exp(-(1.14 -x)/1.2), where λ P B ≈ 1.2 nm.
Q9: What two-peak structure appeared for eight Na⁺ ions in the 1100-water droplet at 200 K, and how did the authors interpret it?
  • A: Peaks at 1.2 and 1.3 nm; they were attributed to a rigid-sphere Poisson–Boltzmann double layer
  • B: Peaks at 0 and 3.8 nm; they corresponded to the ion in the core and at the droplet surface, respectively
  • C: Peaks at 0.52 and 1.5 nm; the inner lower-intensity peak represents one ion exchanging with ions contributing to the outer peak, not a metastable state
  • D: Peaks at 0.52 and 1.5 nm; the inner peak was assigned to a permanently trapped ion and a metastable intermediate

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The two observed maxima were at 0.52 and 1.5 nm; exchange of the single ion associated with the inner peak precludes interpreting the bimodality as metastability.

In droplets comprising 1100 H 2 O molecules -8 Na + ions two peaks are observed at distance 0.52 nm and at 1.5 nm.
Q10: Why does the paper conclude that nonlinear Poisson–Boltzmann predictions cannot explain the multiple-ion distribution under supercooling?
  • A: At supercooling the dielectric constant becomes exactly one, eliminating all electrostatic interactions
  • B: The simulated droplets contain no subsurface layer and therefore violate spherical symmetry
  • C: The Poisson–Boltzmann model predicts ion evaporation, while simulations prohibit all surface charge
  • D: The low-density tetrahedral core expels multiple ions into the disordered subsurface, so the distribution is governed by heterogeneous solvent structure

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors attribute the failure of the NPB description at low temperature to the tetrahedrally organized low-density core, which shifts multiple ions toward the higher-density, disordered subsurface.

Because of the manner in which the H 2 O molecules are organized in the droplet's core at supercooling, the distribution of multiple ions cannot be explained by the predictions of the non-linear Poisson-Boltzmann equation.

Learning design rules for selective oxidation catalysts from highthroughput experimentation and artificial intelligence

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What experimental corpus was used to derive the initial subgroup rules for propylene oxidation?
  • A: 120 SiO2-supported three-component catalysts based on Ru, W, and P, tested across 200–400°C
  • B: 746 four- and five-component catalysts tested only at 400°C
  • C: 120 Mo/V oxide catalysts tested for n-butane oxidation
  • D: 90 catalysts supported on alumina and tested from 25–100°C

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

Mechanochemical synthesis of eco-friendly fertilizer from eggshell (calcite) and KH2PO4

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What reagent pair and solvent-related feature define the proposed mechanochemical fertilizer synthesis?
  • A: Struvite and KH2PO4 react under hydrothermal conditions
  • B: Eggshell calcite and phosphoric acid react in aqueous solution
  • C: Eggshell calcite and KH2PO4 react by solvent-free mechanochemical activation
  • D: Hydroxyapatite and KOH react in an organic solvent

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The study uses eggshell waste as a CaCO3 source and solid KH2PO4 without solvent.

The present article, based on reverse logistic, provides an environmentally beneficial and cost-effective method of producing calcium phosphate bioceramics (potassium calcium phosphates) aimed for soil conditioning, by a free-solvent mechanochemical process between eggshell waste and KH2PO4.
Q2: What milling conditions first produced identifiable potassium-calcium phosphate phases by XRD?
  • A: 1 h at 200 rpm, producing hydroxyapatite as the sole phase
  • B: 15 min at 100 rpm, producing only CaKPO4
  • C: 24 h at 100 rpm, producing only unreacted precursors
  • D: At least 2 h and 400 rpm, chiefly K3CaH(PO4)2 and possibly CaKPO4

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Below 2 h and 400 rpm the patterns showed only reactants; the stated minimum conditions produced phosphate phases.

The milling process between KH2PO4 and eggshell (98% of CaCO3) performed from 2 h and 400 rpm produce a mixture of phosphates, mainly K3CaH(PO4)2 (PDF database n° 22-1218 indicated by black columns, and black dash lines guiding the comparison with the samples) and possibly CaKPO4 (PDF database n° 33-1002 indicated by red columns, and red dash lines guiding the comparison with the samples). Bellow these milling parameters (time and milling speed) only the reactants diffraction patterns are found, i.e., no evidence of chemical reaction is observed.
Q3: How did extended milling affect the phase chemistry relative to the initial mechanochemical products?
  • A: Longer milling initiated secondary reactions including dehydroxylation; CaKPO4 became more evident after 24 h
  • B: Only a 2-h treatment produced CaKPO4, while longer times prevented it
  • C: Longer milling restored calcite and removed all phosphate products
  • D: Milling time had no effect once the initial phosphate appeared

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The paper connects increased milling time with secondary transformations and reports more visible CaKPO4 at 24 h.

When milling time increases, secondary reactions begin, for example, mechanochemical dehydroxylation of the new formed phosphates.
Q4: What transformation was associated with heating the milled phosphate mixture to 420 °C?
  • A: No structural change; only physically adsorbed water was removed
  • B: Conversion to KCaPO4 through recrystallization and partial thermal dehydration of K3CaH(PO4)2
  • C: Complete conversion to hydroxyapatite through hydration
  • D: Formation of CaCO3 by carbonation of KCaPO4

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

XRD after 420 °C showed KCaPO4, linked to partial dehydration/dimerization of the hydrogen phosphate phase.

On the other hand, the thermal treatment at 420°C converted the samples to KCaPO4 (PDF database n° 33-1002), which confirms that the related mass loss is associated to recrystallization by the partial thermal dehydration of the K3CaH(PO4)2 phase that may still be present in the samples.
Q5: How did phosphorus and potassium release kinetics change after mechanochemical processing in 2 wt% citric acid?
  • A: The precursor and milled material had identical rates and model fits
  • B: Potassium release accelerated while phosphorus became undetectable
  • C: Both nutrients showed slower release and better fit pseudo-second-order kinetics than the precursor
  • D: Both nutrients released faster and fit only intraparticle diffusion

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Milling formed slower-dissolving potassium-calcium phosphates, and P and K curves correlated best with pseudo-second-order models.

But, phosphorus and potassium presented better correlation with pseudo-second order model, considering 'R 2 ' and the values related to concentration at equilibrium time "qe" and "experimental qe" (Table 1 ). For both nutrients there was a substantial decrease of the kinetic constant from KH2PO4 to milled samples, attesting the delay in nutrient supply after mechanochemical processing and certainly related to the formation of potassium calcium phosphate phases (K3CaH(PO4)2 and KCaPO4).
Q6: What contrasting effect did milling have on calcium availability relative to the eggshell precursor?
  • A: Calcium became available only after the material was calcined at 710 °C
  • B: Milling suppressed calcium release while accelerating only phosphorus
  • C: Calcium release was identical for eggshell and milled samples
  • D: The new phosphate phases made calcium more readily available, unlike slowly dissolving eggshell CaCO3

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Calcium release was opposite to P and K trends: the new phases increased Ca bioavailability compared with the calcite precursor.

Even not possible to compare kinetic constants for the calcium release, it is important to highlight that in this case was observed an opposite effect: the new phosphates phases from milling made calcium readily available. Thus, considering the kinetic behavior, it was observed that these materials would promote better nutrient management by delaying the bioavailability of phosphorus and potassium (which are highly soluble from KH2PO4) and increasing the calcium bioavailability.
Q7: How did the 2 h/600 rpm sample's phosphorus release in soil compare numerically with KH2PO4?
  • A: It reached 25 mg/kg at day 3 and 45 mg/kg at day 30, versus KH2PO4 at 107 mg/kg by day 3
  • B: It reached 107 mg/kg by day 3, versus KH2PO4 at 45 mg/kg by day 30
  • C: Both materials reached 100 mg/kg within 3 days
  • D: It released no P by day 30, while KH2PO4 reached 25 mg/kg

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The milled sample released P more gradually and recovered about 15% by day 30 versus about 40% of KH2PO4 P after 3 days.

The temporal profile for the precursor KH2PO4 indicates a P increase from 0 mg/kg to 107 mg/kg after 3 days of release keeping constant after 30 days, representing 40% of total P from precursor (KH2PO4). Differently, for sample 2 h/600 rpm, the phosphorus release is more progressive (from 0 mg/kg to 25 mg/kg at 3 days and 45 mg/kg at 30 days) corresponding to 15% of total P content recovered from the milled sample.
Q8: At day 2, how did P radial diffusion for the 2 h/600 rpm sample compare with struvite and KH2PO4?
  • A: It exceeded both materials at 3.5 cm
  • B: It reached 1.99 cm, between struvite's no-release result and KH2PO4's 2.62 cm
  • C: It matched struvite at zero and remained below detection
  • D: It reached 2.62 cm, exceeding KH2PO4 at 1.99 cm

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The day-2 diffusion front for the milled sample was intermediate between the low-release struvite and highly soluble KH2PO4.

At day 2 it is possible to see that struvite was not able to release any phosphorus, while sample 2h/600rpm the P diffusion reach a value of 1.99 cm a significant value compared to 2.62 cm for KH2PO4, a source of P considered extremely soluble.
Q9: What soil-phase mechanism did the authors propose for the slower P and Ca release of the milled material?
  • A: K3CaH(PO4)2 converted to soluble KH2PO4 within the first three days
  • B: Struvite formed and removed calcium from solution
  • C: Hydroxyapatite formed from 3 to 30 days and served as a long-term Ca/P source under acidic soil conditions
  • D: Calcite re-precipitated and permanently sequestered all potassium

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

XRD identified hydroxyapatite in soil residues; the paper describes its slow-release behavior and solubilization with acidification near pH 5.

The slower release for calcium and phosphorus for 2 h/600 rpm sample and the P radial diffusion are probably related to hydroxyapatite formation -(Ca10(PO4)6(OH)2) (PDF database n° 9-432 indicated by green columns) -identified from 3 until 30 days in soil conditions (Fig. 7 ).
Q10: What was the main caveat when comparing soil-release behavior with the citric-acid assay?
  • A: The soil assay used a different product and therefore could not be compared
  • B: Soil release was always faster, so citric acid could not predict trends
  • C: Soil results showed rapid P and K release but no measurable calcium
  • D: The soil experiments showed the same qualitative trend but generally slower release than 2 wt% citric acid

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors explicitly note the same behavior but an overall slower profile in soil, plausibly connected with hydroxyapatite formation.

The soil essays performed indicate the same behavior, but a general slower release trend when compared to citric acid solution (wt 2%) essays, this can be explained by hydroxyapatite (Ca10(PO4)6(OH)2) formation, that still an important source of calcium and phosphorus as a long-term slow release fertilizer.

Metal-Free Photocatalysis at Charged Aqueous Interface: Boosting the Photocatalytic Oxidative Coupling of Arylamines to Azoaromatics under Ambient Conditions †

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Why did the authors select EY@CTAB rather than EY@TX-100 for the subsequent photocatalysis?
  • A: EY binds electrostatically to positively charged CTAB, and EY@TX-100 disassembles in K3PO4
  • B: TX-100 produced the largest lifetime increase and resisted disassembly
  • C: SDS enhanced EY fluorescence more than CTAB and remained stable in base
  • D: CTAB prevented EY association, while TX-100 concentrated the amine substrate

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

EY is dianionic at pH 7, shows CTAB-specific spectral/lifetime evidence of association, and the TX-100 composite is unstable in base.

These findings suggest a higher binding affinity of EY towards CTAB (EY@CTAB) than TX-100 (EY@TX-100). This is expected as EY bears two negative charges at pH 7 32 and hence, interacts electrostatically with the positively charged CTAB. At the premicellar concentration of CTAB, EY undergoes CTAB-induced aggregation due to ion-pair formation; however, these aggregates subsequently dissociate at or near the CMC of CTAB due to entrapment of EY at the micellar Stern layer (Figure 1c ). 33, 34 This argument gains further support from the enhanced fluorescence lifetime of EY from 1.17 ns in water to 2.74 ns upon micellization of CTAB (Figure 1d and Table S2 ), signifying micellar association. As the present POC reaction occurs in the presence of a base, we checked the stabilities of the micellar composites of EY in the presence of K3PO4. Although EY@CTAB is found to be stable in the presence of K3PO4, EY@TX-100 suffers disassembly (Figure S2 and S3 ). Therefore, we utilized the EY@CTAB composite for our subsequent studies.
Q2: What specific fluorescence and spectral evidence distinguished CTAB micelles from SDS in the initial EY screening?
  • A: Both surfactants quenched fluorescence equally across all concentrations
  • B: CTAB produced a 2.4-fold fluorescence enhancement at 0.75–1.0 mM near CMC, while SDS showed no spectral change
  • C: SDS produced a 2.4-fold enhancement, while CTAB showed no peak shift
  • D: CTAB shifted emission by 16 nm while SDS doubled the fluorescence lifetime

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The reported CTAB response near its CMC contrasted with the lack of spectral changes for SDS.

However, EY shows enhanced fluorescence (2.4-fold) only in the presence of CTAB at and near (0.75-1.0 mM) the critical micellar concentration (CMC). No such spectral changes have been observed in the presence of SDS (Figure 1a, b ), signifying lack of any interaction.
Q3: Under the optimized p-anisidine conditions, what combination yielded complete conversion in four hours?
  • A: Green light, oxygen-free solvent, 8.3 mol% EY, and 40 mM SDS
  • B: Blue light, nitrogen, 5 mol% EY, no base, and 4 mM CTAB
  • C: Green light at 525 nm, ambient air, 0.6 mol% EY, 2 equivalents K3PO4, and 40 mM CTAB
  • D: UV light, 0.6 mol% EY, 1 equivalent DABCO, and TX-100

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The stated standard reaction uses a low EY loading and the CTAB micellar scaffold under air.

The reaction was triggered by a green light (λ = 525 nm) under ambient air atmosphere and within 4 h we observed 100% conversion to desired azo product in the presence of 0.6 mol% EY, 2 equiv. K3PO4, and 40 mM CTAB as determined from GC-MS measurements (Table 1 ).
Q4: What was the performance cost of using blue rather than green irradiation under the reported standard conditions?
  • A: It prevented the reaction unless TX-100 replaced CTAB
  • B: It required an inert atmosphere and 8.3-fold more catalyst
  • C: It reduced conversion to 64% after 24 h
  • D: It still gave 100% conversion but required 6 h instead of 4 h

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The authors report feasibility under blue light but a longer time to full conversion.

The reaction is also feasible with the blue light as reported previously 24 ; however, requires 6 h for 100% conversion under standard conditions (Figure S5 ).
Q5: What role does atmospheric oxygen play in the photocatalytic cycle according to the authors' control experiment?
  • A: It serves as sacrificial oxidant and is reduced to H2O2, regenerating the photocatalyst
  • B: It suppresses radical formation by oxidizing EY before excitation
  • C: It acts as the base that abstracts a proton from the arylamine
  • D: It forms the azo product directly without an electron-transfer step

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

Hydrogen peroxide sensing strips changed after irradiation, supporting oxygen reduction during photocatalyst regeneration.

Control experiments were performed with H2O2 sensing strips to detect the formation of H2O2 in the reaction mixture (Figure S6 ). The distinct color change of the strip observed before and after the light irradiation indicates the formation of H2O2 in the reaction mixture via reduction of aerial O2. Therefore, atmospheric O2 acts as a sacrificial oxidant and involved in the regeneration of the PC via formation of H2O2 as byproduct.
Q6: What mechanistic interpretation follows from the fluorescence-quenching rate measured for p-anisidine?
  • A: The lifetime remains unchanged, indicating only ground-state proton transfer
  • B: A kq of 6.3×10^12 M−1s−1 and 0.3-ps PET support non-diffusive interfacial electron transfer
  • C: The lack of a linear Stern–Volmer plot rules out PET
  • D: A kq below 10^6 M−1s−1 indicates diffusion-limited transfer over seconds

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

Both the unusually fast quenching rate and ultrafast up-conversion time constant are presented as evidence for non-diffusive PET.

A linear Stern-Volmer (SV) plot with a quenching rate constant (kq) of 6.3 × 10 12 M -1 s -1 suggests a non-diffusive bimolecular quenching process (Figure 2b ). 37, 38 Quenching parameters for other arylamines are tabulated in Table S3 . Notably, lack of fluorescence quenching with p-nitroaniline suggests lack of any electron transfer to EY*@CTAB (Figure 2b ). It is noteworthy to mention that the presence of K3PO4 has no noticeable effect on the SV plots and quenching rate constant (Figure S14 ), signifying that PET precedes the proton abstraction step by base. (Figure 2c and Table S4 ). Although PET at the micellar surface is instantaneous in nature and occurs in a few picoseconds, [39] [40] [41] [42] the marked decrease in the fluorescence lifetime of EY@CTAB authenticates the excited state quenching by p-anisidine. Fluorescence up-conversion measurements reveal an ultrafast PET with a time constant of 0.3 ps in the presence of p-anisidine (Figure 2d ).

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

A characteristic HR-MS peak for p-anisidine-BHT adducts appears at a molecular weight of 342.2399 g/mol (Figure S15 ). Moreover, the presence of BHT results in negligible conversion (~5.0%) to the desired azo product even after 4 h

  • D: Electron-rich substrates and aniline reached 100%, whereas p-chloro- and p-bromoaniline reached 98% and 95%

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

A Workflow for Identifying Viable Crystal Structures with Partially Occupied Sites Applied to Solid Electrolyte: Cubic Li 7 La 3 Zr 2 O 12

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Why was brute-force enumeration of stoichiometric c-LLZO configurations infeasible, and what was the initial combinatorial count?
  • A: The count is 2×10^6 because all configurations are already symmetry unique
  • B: There are only 120 possible arrangements, but each requires a full geometry optimization
  • C: Populating 120 sites with 56 Li gives about 7.4×10^34 arrangements
  • D: Populating 56 sites with 120 Li gives 2.3×10^8 arrangements

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The enormous raw occupancy count motivated geometric and symmetry-based pruning before energy ranking.

The number of ways to populate 120 sites with 56 Li is 120 56 ≈ 7.4 × 10 34 , rendering a brute-force combinatorial method unviable.
Q2: What does the 1.7 Å Li–Li exclusion constraint imply for the partially occupied 96h sites?
  • A: Each 24d site excludes exactly one 96h site, allowing 56 total Li on 96h
  • B: All 96h sites can be occupied if no 24d site is occupied
  • C: The constraint eliminates every 24d site and leaves 24 occupied 96h sites
  • D: At most 48 of the paired 96h sites can be occupied, and each occupied 24d site excludes four 96h sites

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Each 96h pair is only 0.68 Å apart, and the stated cutoff also excludes neighboring 96h sites around an occupied 24d position.

1. A maximum of 48 out of 96h sites can be occupied at anyone time: each 96h site exists as a pair within 0.68 Å of each other, at most one site can contain a Li atom. 2. Each of the 24d occupation eliminates 4 out of 96h sites as they are within 1.67 Å of this site.
Q3: How did the structure-generation stage avoid forbidden nearest-neighbor Li overlaps?
  • A: It enumerated 24d permutations first, removed adjacent 96h candidates, then filled 96h without simultaneous nearest-neighbor occupation
  • B: It generated only the 48g site and inferred the 24d/96h occupancies afterward
  • C: It placed all 96h Li randomly and relaxed overlaps with molecular dynamics
  • D: It used diffraction intensities to choose one experimentally observed configuration

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The procedure applies the geometric constraint during enumeration, before symmetry reduction.

We initially generate all possible 24d permutations for a given 24d occupancy, eliminating directly neighbouring 96h sites from consideration. We then populate the remaining 96h sites such that no two nearest neighbours are occupied simultaneously.
Q4: What representation and search method were used to reduce symmetry-equivalent configurations efficiently?
  • A: A full Cartesian-coordinate comparison of every pair using conventional O(N²) search
  • B: A sorted flattened upper-triangular Li–Li distance matrix with local sensitive hashing
  • C: A La–Zr interaction matrix ranked by a neural network
  • D: A reciprocal-space diffraction fingerprint with principal-component analysis

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The Li sublattice connectivity matrix stores pairwise distances, and LSH accelerates the approximate symmetry similarity searches.

To further reduce our large data set to unique conformations, we construct the upper triangular connectivity matrix of the Li sub-lattice for each structure. We define a connectivity matrix as the distances between each Li atom to every other Li atom. The matrix is then flattened and sorted to ensure uniformity in all matrices.
Q5: What were the scale and trade-off of the local-sensitive-hashing symmetry reduction?
  • A: It guaranteed exact symmetry for arbitrary atomic species with no approximation
  • B: It only worked for 20 structures and did not reduce memory
  • C: It reduced pairwise scaling from O(N²) to O(N), but its high specificity trades away some completeness
  • D: It reduced O(N) to O(N²), improving sensitivity at the cost of memory

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The method is fast and memory efficient for large similar datasets but is approximate/high-specificity and limited to predefined monoatomic sublattices.

The use of LSH optimises the pairwise comparison from scaling at O(N 2 ) to O(N ), where N is the number of structures. This symmetry comparison method is designed to work for large data sets of similar data, allowing us to perform symmetry checks on over 100 million structures in a reasonable time frame and attainable memory requirements, something that would be impractical to attempt with conventional methods. [48] [49] [50] The trade-off for increased speed and lower memory costs is the high specificity of the method. In practice, this method is limited to comparison of monoatomic sub-lattices with predefined positions.
Q6: How many configurations remained after the two main reductions, and what fraction were symmetry unique?
  • A: The configuration count was unchanged because symmetry duplicates were retained
  • B: Only 200 remained after geometry optimization
  • C: About 99% were unique, leaving 2.3×10^8 configurations from 2×10^6
  • D: About 1% were unique, leaving roughly 2×10^6 configurations from an initial 2.3×10^8

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The geometric enumeration yielded around 230 million structures, and symmetry hashing reduced them by about a factor of 100.

This method will generate all possible structures, approximately 2.3 × 10 8 , including symmetry-equivalent duplicates.
Q7: How were the DFT training structures selected for the multiple-linear-regression energy model?
  • A: The 200 lowest COMPASSIII structures plus about 1,000 random structures spanning the space, for 1,235 DFT points total
  • B: A random sample of 200 structures, all from the 8:48 ratio
  • C: Every one of the approximately two million structures received a DFT calculation
  • D: The 1,235 lowest-energy structures according to ONETEP only

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The two subsets combine a low-energy-biased sample and broad random coverage to train an energy predictor affordably.

The DFT calculations were performed on 2 major subsets of structures. The first subset was the 200 energetically lowest structures according to the COMPASSIII forcefield, 51 (c.f. SI section ). The second subset was a random selection of approximately 1000 structures across the entire configuration space to ensure good coverage. In total 1235 single point DFT calculations were performed.
Q8: What descriptor did the MLR model use, and how well did it correlate with held-out DFT energies?
  • A: Total Li count and lattice volume, with correlation 0.83
  • B: Frequencies of possible Li–Li interactions, with a test-set correlation of 0.9996
  • C: Space-group number alone, with correlation 0.991
  • D: A neural embedding of La/Zr sites, with correlation 0.58

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The pair-interaction frequency list reformulates the Li connectivity matrix and was strongly predictive on the held-out set.

The structures were represented by a frequency occurrence list of all possible Li-Li interactions. This representation is a reformulation of the connectivity matrix of the Li sub-lattice used for the symmetry reduction described above. The data were split into test and training sets with a 1:3 ratio. We found through applying this model to our test set a correlation of 0.9996
Q9: What occupancy pattern dominated the low-energy structures, and what energetic separation did the authors report?
  • A: The 9:47 ratio dominated and was 0.58 eV below all 8:48 structures
  • B: All ratios were equally represented within 5.8 meV
  • C: Nineteen of the 20 lowest were 8:48, with four such structures within 0.026 eV of the minimum
  • D: Nineteen of the 20 lowest were 12:44, all separated by over 1.47 eV

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The low-energy ranking strongly favors low 24d occupation and identifies four near-degenerate 8:48 structures.

19 out of the 20 lowest energy structures have a ratio of 8:48 (c.f. Figure 2b ) with the 19 th lowest being 9:47. The average energy for all structures occurs at 1.47 eV higher than the ground state structures. The average energy for each ratio increases with increasing 24d occupancy, indicating an energetic preference to avoid 24d occupation where possible. There are four structures, all 8:48, within 0.026 eV (1 kT at 298 K) of the lowest energy structure we find
Q10: Which limitations qualify the study's energetic ranking of candidate structures?
  • A: It includes full entropy but assumes the structure never relaxes
  • B: It uses only experimental energies and excludes geometric constraints
  • C: It optimizes all two million configurations and therefore has no model uncertainty
  • D: It omits entropic contributions and assumes ordering is reasonably retained under relaxation; only a small relaxation subset was tested

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors explicitly flag entropy and ordering retention as approximations and note that broad energy prediction after relaxation is outside scope.

We note that our energetic ordering procedure does not include entropic contributions and assumes a reasonable retention of ordering upon geometry relaxation, which are both approximations. We have tested the effect of geometry relaxation on a small dataset of 20 structures (c.f. SI section ). A large-scale energy prediction of the geometry optimised structures falls outside the scope of this communication.

Self-assembling materials mimicking dynamic bio-interfaces in cellular activities

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: How did the two glycine substitutions in RADA16 alter fibre morphology and hydrogel mechanics?
  • A: Middle-residue A8G formed bundled fibres and a more robust gel, whereas terminal A16G formed curved fibres and a softer, thixotropic gel
  • B: A8G formed curved fibres and a softer gel, whereas A16G formed bundled fibres and a stiffer gel
  • C: Both mutants formed identical straight fibres, but A16G had lower biocompatibility
  • D: A8G prevented gelation, whereas A16G formed a non-thixotropic hydrogel stiffer than RADA16

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The review contrasts the middle alanine-to-glycine mutant A8G with the terminal mutant A16G: flexibility does not produce the same supramolecular morphology or macroscopic stiffness in each position.

A8G, a mutant of RADA16 in which the alanine in the middle of the peptide sequence is replaced with glycine, forms bundled nanofibres and a more robust hydrogel than RADA16
Q2: What feature of the A16G-N-cadherin construct accounted for its enhanced neuroblast migration relative to A16G alone?
  • A: N-cadherin was mixed freely with the hydrogel, and the effect was independent of peptide tagging
  • B: N-cadherin was tagged to the self-assembling A16G peptide, displaying a migration-relevant adhesion cue on the fibrous scaffold
  • C: A16G was covalently crosslinked to RADA16, increasing gel stiffness without presenting a ligand
  • D: N-cadherin was replaced with VEGF, which induced neuroblast proliferation rather than migration

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The A16G-Ncad conjugate couples a self-assembling peptide scaffold with N-cadherin, which is implicated in neuroblast migration along radial glial fibres.

Time-lapse imaging of ventricular-subventricular zone neuroblasts on culture dishes coated with A16G or a mixture of A16G and A16G-Ncad hydrogels showed that the migration rate of neuroblasts was significantly higher on the hydrogel containing A16G-Ncad than on A16G alone
Q3: What molecular packing motif enables JigSAP's one-dimensional self-assembly?
  • A: A histidine-rich motif coordinates Cu²⁺ at the membrane edge and drives phospholipid sheet assembly
  • B: A GXXXG motif remains permanently α-helical and forms fibres by covalent disulfide crosslinks
  • C: An AXXXA hydrophobic motif undergoes an α-helix-to-β-strand transition and dovetail-like dimerization, promoting β-sheet nanofibre formation

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

JigSAP contains an AXXXA motif; the conformational transition exposes a jigsaw-like hydrophobic surface that dimerizes by dovetail packing and supports β-sheet fibre assembly.

These motifs show α-helix-to-β-strand conformational transitions, generating a jigsaw-shaped hydrophobic surface that dimerizes with dovetail packing.
Q4: How did JigSAP compare with 5V and RADA16 in protein incorporation and subsequent release?
  • A: EGFP-JigSAP incorporation was 66 mol%; after 168 h it released 5 mol%, versus 39 mol% for 5V
  • B: Non-tagged EGFP incorporation was 93 mol%; all three gels released over 50 mol% by 24 h
  • C: JigSAP incorporation was 3 mol%; after 168 h it released 8 mol%, matching RADA16

EGFP-JigSAP is incorporated at 93 mol%, whereas non-tagged EGFP is incorporated at only 3 mol%
Q5: What biochemical distinction between JigSAP-IMI/ MII and their oxidized forms produced a stimulus-responsive gel-to-sol transition?

remain dispersed in solution under a physiological condition (4 mM HEPES, pH 7.4, DMEM containing 1.0 wt% peptide concentration

GUIDEMOL: a Python graphical user interface for molecular descriptors based on RDKit

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Which exact RDKit routine and optimization settings does GUIDEMOL use to generate 3D structures from SMILES?
  • A: AddHs followed by Chem.rdDistGeom with a 5000 kcal/mol cutoff
  • B: EmbedMolecule with ETKDGv3 followed by UFF for 500 iterations
  • C: MMFFOptimizeMolecule with MMFF94 and maxIters=5000
  • D: ConstrainedEmbed followed by MMFF94s for 1000 iterations

Gold: C · No context: B · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper names the optimization call, force-field variant and iteration limit.

The 3D molecular models are obtained with the RDKit function MMFFOptimizeMolecule and the arguments mmffVariant='MMFF94' and maxIters=5000.
Q2: Which preprocessing choices can be applied before generating the models from SMILES?
  • A: Aromatize rings, retain only the smallest fragment, and protonate all heteroatoms
  • B: Remove salts, force a single tautomer, and assign MMFF atom types
  • C: Neutralize, canonicalize stereochemistry, and delete all explicit hydrogens
  • D: Remove metals, retain the largest preferably organic fragment, and neutralize

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The three listed optional steps are metal disconnection, largest-fragment selection, and uncharging.

to remove metals (based on the MetalDisconnector class), to keep the largest fragment (uses LargestFragmentChooser(preferOrganic=True)), or to neutralize (uses Uncharger).
Q3: Which descriptor families are explicitly exposed from RDKit's rdMolDescriptors module in the interface?
  • A: RDF, MORSE, WHIM, AUTOCORR3D, GETAWAY, PEOE_VSA and SMR_VSA
  • B: Morgan, MACCS, Avalon, ErG, AtomPair and TopologicalTorsion
  • C: BCUT, Kappa, Chi, Hall-Kier, LabuteASA and EState
  • D: RDF, MORSE, WHIM, AUTOCORR3D, PEOE_VSA, Morgan and MACCS

Gold: A · No context: B · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

These are the seven listed 3D descriptor families; exact molecular weight is separately selectable.

RDF, MORSE, WHIM, AUTOCORR3D, GETAWAY, PEOE_VSA and SMR_VSA.
Q4: In the electrostatic-potential grid, how are atoms very close to a cube center treated in the stated equation?
  • A: They are replaced by their atomic number before potential summation
  • B: They are excluded when r<1 Å, and their contribution equals their partial charge
  • C: They are assigned zero charge and excluded only if also beyond the cutoff
  • D: They are retained with a Coulomb term capped at 1/r=1 Å

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The method excludes atoms at distance less than 1 Å from the cube center while specifying their contribution as their partial charge.

the atoms at a distance < 1 Å from the center of the cube (the contribution is equal to its partial charge)
Q5: Which pair of user-set spatial parameters is used to specify a potential-grid calculation, in addition to the cutoff?
  • A: Center-of-mass coordinates and dielectric constant, with side length setting cutoff
  • B: Grid origin and number of molecules, with sigma controlling resolution
  • C: Grid-center coordinates and side length, with cube size setting resolution
  • D: Molecular centroid and atom count, with a fixed 1 Å voxel width

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Users set the center coordinates, grid side size, and cube size; the cutoff determines which distant atoms contribute.

The coordinates of the center of the grid, the size of each side (in Angstrom) and the size of the cube are also defined by the user.
Q6: How does GUIDEMOL form each voxel-grid value from atom-specific properties?
  • A: It averages pᵢ/rᵢc over all atoms and normalizes by molecular mass
  • B: It sums pᵢ exp(−rᵢc/(2σ)) over atoms closer than 1 Å
  • C: It assigns the nearest atom's property, then smooths by a Coulomb kernel
  • D: It sums pᵢ exp(−rᵢc²/(2σ²)) over atoms within the cutoff

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The voxel representation uses a Gaussian distance weighting of the selected atomic property and omits atoms beyond the cutoff.

VoxG c = ∑ i=1 N p i ×exp ( -r ic 2 2 σ 2 )
Q7: Which set consists entirely of atomic properties implemented for voxelization?
  • A: Element atomic number, partial charge, and Wildman–Crippen atomic contributions to refractivity and logP
  • B: Element atomic number, electronegativity, polarizability and Gasteiger–Marsili bond order
  • C: MMFF energy, refractivity, aromaticity and molecular dipole contribution
  • D: Partial charge, atomic mass, solvent-accessible area and topological degree

Gold: A · No context: B · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The implementation supports atomic number, Gasteiger/MMFF charge, and Crippen refractivity/logP contributions.

Implemented properties are the atomic number of the element, the partial atomic charge (Gasteiger or MMFF) and atomic contributions to molecular refractivity and logP using the Wildman-Crippen approach
Q8: What limitation did the authors report for selected 3D descriptors across repeated runs or operating systems?
  • A: All RDF and PEOE_VSA values changed because GUIDEMOL randomized atomic charges
  • B: Some GETAWAY, MORSE and WHIM values differed despite the same RDKit version and generated structure
  • C: Voxel and potential grids were nondeterministic, while every RDKit descriptor remained invariant
  • D: Only exact molecular weight varied because the GUI rounded masses by operating system

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

for some molecules, and some GETAWAY, MORSE and WHIM descriptors, different values were obtained with the same RDKit version (2022.9.5) in different runs or in different operating systems.
Q9: When hydrogens are added to an existing 3D input structure, what coordinate-handling behavior does the option specify?
  • A: All atoms are re-embedded, with existing heavy-atom coordinates used only as restraints
  • B: Only hydrogens missing from aromatic groups are added, with coordinates left undefined
  • C: Hydrogen coordinates are generated without changing other atoms' coordinates, using AddHs(addCoords=True)
  • D: Hydrogens are placed at idealized positions and the structure is subsequently minimized

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The optional operation adds missing H coordinates while leaving the supplied atoms unmoved.

The descriptors are calculated from the 3D structure supplied by the user. However, an option is available for the addition of missing hydrogen atoms, for which the 3D coordinates are calculated without changing the coordinates of the other atoms
Q10: What does the article say about the BSD license and intended extensibility of GUIDEMOL?
  • A: Its GPL license requires all derived descriptor workflows to be open source
  • B: Its BSD license allows descriptor calculations but prohibits reuse of the tkinter interface
  • C: Its research-only license permits classroom use but forbids commercial modification

The authors explicitly describe both the license scope and the code's adaptability.

under the three-clause Berkeley Software Distribution (BSD) license, which allows both commercial and non-commercial use, and can be very easily extended for the deployment of other molecular descriptors

A Single-domain Protein Catenane of Dihydrofolate Reductase

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: How did the authors select the split site for the two cat-DHFR rings?
  • A: They chose the α-helix-C/β-sheet-5 boundary at residues 88–89, with GLN +0.726
  • B: They chose the active-site center at residues 23–24 because it had GLN zero
  • C: They selected a structured hydrophobic core site with the lowest linking number
  • D: They split the enzyme at residue 159 to create two equal rings

Gold: A · No context: C · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The chosen loop-region split preserved the active site and gave highly intertwined segments according to the GLN analysis.

Combining these criteria, we identified that cleavage at the position between the α-helix-C and β-sheet-5 (residues 88 and 89) would generate two highly intertwined segments (GLN +0.726, Figure S1a ), which could be converted to catenane topology upon cyclization of the two fragments.
Q2: What chain segments form the two rings, and what GLN did the completed catenane attain?
  • A: Two identical 1–159 chains form rings with GLN zero
  • B: Residues 1–88 and 89–159 form the rings; cat-DHFR has GLN +1.120
  • C: Residues 1–89 and 90–159 form the rings; cat-DHFR has GLN −1.120
  • D: Residues 1–23 and 24–159 form the rings; cat-DHFR has GLN +0.726

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The adopted design divides the enzyme into the two complementary chains and the resulting catenane has greater intertwinement than the virtual split.

Thus, a design composed of residues 1-88 as one ring and residues 89-159 as the other ring was adopted (Figure 1a ). The two fragments are known to reconstitute efficiently. 42, 45 The entangling core of the design is similar to that of the wild-type and the linker naturally extends to form the ring, giving a cat-DHFR with a GLN of +1.120 (Figure S1b ).
Q3: Why was ring I circularly permutated to make new termini between residues 23 and 24?
  • A: The active site had to be split between residues 23 and 24 to restore DHFR catalysis
  • B: The termini needed to remain adjacent so both inteins could bind on the same side
  • C: The two bulky split-intein pairs could interfere, so placing closure motifs on opposite sides improved cyclization geometry
  • D: The permutation shortened the loop to zero residues and increased aggregation

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The design accounts for distances between termini and steric interference of the two split-intein pairs.

Considering that cyclization efficiency depends on the distance between the new termini and that the two bulky split-intein pairs may interfere with each other, ring I was circularly permutated to generate new termini between residues 23 and 24, and the rewired loops were designed to be long enough ( 16residues) to bridge the split structural motifs.
Q4: What additional element was encoded in the first ring specifically to test the catenane topology?
  • A: A fluorescent tag whose emission reports the number of rings
  • B: A His-tag duplicated in both rings to force disulfide formation
  • C: A restriction site designed to cleave the peptide backbone in vitro
  • D: A TEV protease recognition sequence whose cleavage should yield one linear and one cyclic fragment

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The TEV site enables a topology-sensitive proteolysis assay: cleavage opens one ring but leaves the other cyclic.

The gene cassette was then designed containing two DHFR segments as the entangling template, two mutually orthogonal split-intein pairs for ring closure, and the tobacco etch virus (TEV) protease recognition sequence in the first ring for subsequent proof of topology by proteolytic digestion experiment
Q5: What indirect topology test was used after the authors could not obtain suitable cat-DHFR crystals?
  • A: TEV cleavage produced the predicted linear and cyclic fragments, confirmed by SDS-PAGE and LC-MS
  • B: Only a far-UV CD spectrum was used to establish mechanical interlocking
  • C: X-ray diffraction resolved both linked rings at atomic resolution
  • D: The protein was reduced to two identical linear fragments by DTT

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: Why did the study use separate reference levels of theory for periodic and non-periodic systems?

  • C: Hybrid wB97X-D3BJ was selected for non-periodic systems, while PBE-D3 was used periodically because hybrid periodic calculations were too costly
  • D: Hybrid functionals were chosen for metals because they scale linearly in periodic systems

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The paper pairs high-quality cluster labels with tractable periodic PBE labels, noting the expense of periodic hybrid calculations.

we selected wB97X-D3BJ/def2-TZVPD as our reference level of theory for non-periodic systems and PBE-D3 in a plane wave basis as our reference level of theory for periodic systems. We also labeled a random subset of non-periodic systems with PBE-D3 to improve the information exchange between output heads in our multitask learning scheme. Unfortunately, the great expense of hybrid functionals such as wB97X-D3BJ for periodic systems means that it was not feasible to label our periodic dataset with this level of theory.
Q2: Why did the authors prefer GFN1-xTB partial charges over GFN2-xTB or GFN0-xTB?

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Instead, we tested reference atomic partial charges from GFN1-xTB, GFN2-xTB, and GFN0-xTB [16] [17] [18] , and found the older GFN1-xTB to be the most suitable for generating charge labels for SEI training sets, because it achieves the best compromise between accuracy and convergence across isolated and periodic systems

Q7: What two-stage MD protocol was used to prepare and rank each candidate structure?
  • A: Anneal at 400 K and 1,000 atm for 100 ps, then rank by mean energy over 20 ps at 300 K and 1 atm
  • B: Run 1 ns at 400 K and rank a single instantaneous energy at 0 K
  • C: Optimize with DFT at 1,000 K, then sample at 1 atm for 1 ps
  • D: Anneal at 300 K and 1 atm for 20 ps, then rank at 400 K and 1,000 atm for 100 ps

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The harsh NPT annealing was followed by ambient-condition trajectories for comparable energy ranking.

Having produced a series of unique initial geometries, we then annealed them using our reactive QRNN model with NPT MD at 400 K and 1000 atm for 100 ps each. Remarkably, our QRNN models gave stable dynamics despite the extreme conditions applied, which is often not the case for MLFFs [28] . Finally we ranked each set of candidates by calculating the mean potential energy of each structure over an MD trajectory of 20 ps length at ambient conditions of 300 K and 1 atm.
Q8: What bond-count associations did the authors find in the most lithiated subgroup?
  • A: C–O and Li–F bonds tracked more stable structures, whereas P–F and Li–O tracked less stable ones

  • D: All four bond types were equally associated with total energy

Gold: B · No context: A · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

Bond counts were compared with total system energies after Lewis-structure recalculation for the 33-added-Li group.

We performed statistical analysis of the bonding in SEI candidate structures by recalculating the Lewis structure of the entire system based on the atomic positions after reactive MD using Schrodinger Materials Science Suite [29] , then tabulating the counts of various bond types versus the total system energy (Fig 4 ). P-F and Li-O bonds are associated with more stable structures, while C-O and Li-F bonds are associated with less stable ones.
Q9: Why could the authors not directly validate predicted SEI density, diffusivity, viscosity, or modulus?
  • A: Those quantities were excluded because the SEI has no organic components
  • B: The available experiments contradicted the simulated values but were not reported
  • C: Experimental values for any SEI composition were unavailable, so validation instead used calculated surface reaction barriers
  • D: Their model produced no stable dynamics for any system

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The authors identify the absence of experimental SEI material properties as a validation obstacle and compare to best-available reaction barrier calculations instead.

For example, we cannot compare the density, diffusivity, viscosity, or elastic modulus of our SEI material against experimental values because such experiments do not exist for any SEI composition. Instead, we validated our model against the best available calculations for surface reaction energy barriers
Q10: What scope limitation most directly constrains conclusions about how electrolyte formulation controls SEI structure?
  • A: All predicted candidates were experimentally synthesized and tested
  • B: The work compared five electrolyte formulations but only one charge state
  • C: The training set excluded LiPF6 and EC entirely
  • D: Candidates were built only for 1 M LiPF6 in EC, so comparing multiple formulations remains future work

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The authors caution that formulation comparison is needed before structure/property trends can be optimized across chemistries.

We have so far constructed SEI candidates using only a single formulation of electrolyte, 1M LiPF 6 in EC. Based on this common formulation, we are able to suggest plausible structures for the SEI and analyze what makes a structure more or less energetically stable. However, the full power of this technique will come from comparing multiple formulations.

Highly sensitive and full-genome interrogation of SARS-CoV-2 using multiplexed PCR enrichment followed by next-generation sequencing

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What assumptions produced the modeled 1.15-copy, 95%-probability LOD for the first primer pool?
  • A: 172 primer pairs averaging 99 bp, 6% single-strand cDNA amplification efficiency, and 80% target success
  • B: 28 targets at 99% efficiency and no failed amplicons
  • C: One RT-PCR amplicon at 6% efficiency with 50% genome recovery
  • D: 171 primer pairs averaging 158 bp, 26% efficiency, and 100% target success

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The model combines the 172-pair panel design with conservative amplification and target-recovery assumptions.

We designed a panel of 172 pairs of multiplex PCR primers in order to increase the sensitivity of detecting SARS-CoV-2 (Fig. 1B ). The average amplicon length is 99bp. The amplicons span across the entire SARS-CoV-2 genome with an average 76bp gap (76±10bp) between adjacent amplicons. Since the observed efficiency of multiplex PCR is about 26% in amplifying the four DNA strands of a pair of human chromosomes, we assumed an efficiency of 6% in amplifying the single-strand of a cDNA molecule. In addition, it has already been reported that 79% of variants are recovered when directly amplifying 600 amplicons from a single cell using our technology 37 . Therefore, we assumed that 80% of targeted regions would be amplified successfully. Using the same mathematical model described above, we estimated that our SARS-CoV-2 panel can detect 1.15 copies of the virus at 95% probability
Q2: Why did a second overlapping primer pool improve the modeled detection limit beyond the first pool?
  • A: It doubled the length of every first-pool amplicon and raised efficiency to 99%
  • B: Its 171 pairs targeted gaps in the 172-pair pool, together providing full-genome coverage and a modeled 0.29-copy LOD
  • C: It increased the first pool from 172 to 343 pairs without overlap
  • D: It amplified only the S gene, eliminating the N-gene targets

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The second pool was deliberately complementary to first-pool gaps, increasing the chance of at least one target occurrence.

We also designed a second pool of 171 multiplex PCR primer pairs. The target regions of these primer pairs overlap with the gaps between target regions of the previous pool of 172 primer pairs (Fig. 1B ). Together, these two overlapping pools of primers provide full coverage of the entire viral genome. Most importantly, using both pools in detection would lead to a calculated detection limit of 0.29 copies at 95% probability.
Q3: What role did electrophoresis play relative to sequencing in the diagnostic workflow?
  • A: NGS was used only to size amplicons, while electrophoresis detected mutations
  • B: Electrophoresis alone identified SARS-CoV-2 uniquely, making NGS unnecessary
  • C: Defined-size peaks gave a fast indication of a coronavirus-family positive, while NGS identified the specific virus
  • D: Neither was used until after clinical sequencing was complete

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The authors distinguish rapid amplicon-pattern screening from definitive virus identification by sequencing.

Since dozens of target regions could be amplified from a single copy of SARS-CoV-2, electrophoresis peaks with a defined distribution of peak sizes were expected. Multiplex PCR could potentially amplify not only SARS-CoV-2, but also other coronaviruses, due to shared sequence similarities despite the fact that we designed primers specific to the SARS-CoV-2 genome to avoid cross amplification. In that context, electrophoresis analysis provides a fast and sensitive indication of infection from at least that family of viruses. For specificity, the generated NGS sequencing library can be interrogated for definitive identification of the specific virus.
Q4: How did the single-copy plasmid experiment compare the modeled and observed probability of detecting about 1.1 copies?
  • A: Both model and experiment predicted exactly 100% detection
  • B: The model predicted 26% and no experiment was conducted
  • C: The model predicted 56%, but the experiment observed 12%
  • D: The model predicted 12% detection after sampling probability, but the experiment observed 56%

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The observed detection rate was substantially higher than predicted, indicating the assumed 6% single-strand efficiency was conservative.

The theoretical calculation gives a 66% probability to sample 1.1 copies, and a 12% chance to detect them based on a multiplex PCR efficiency of 6%. In practice, we experimentally observed a significantly higher 56% probability to detect 1.1 copies (Fig. 2C ). These results suggest that the efficiency of multiplex PCR is actually higher than the previously estimated 6% when a single-stranded cDNA molecule is amplified.
Q5: At what plasmid input did library recovery become incomplete, and how steep was the drop?
  • A: About 96% of amplicons at 14 copies, 77% at 2.8 copies, and 37% at 0.6 copies
  • B: About 96% were recovered at 1,700 copies, but no lower-input tests were performed
  • C: About 37% at 14 copies, 77% at 2.8 copies, and 96% at 0.6 copies
  • D: All amplicons were recovered at 0.6 copies and none at 14 copies

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The amplicon recovery fraction decreases sharply at low copy number, despite high sensitivity for detecting a positive signal.

Approximately 96% of the amplicons were recovered with 14 copies of plasmids, 77% with 2.8 copies, and 37% with 0.6 copies (Fig. 2E ).
Q6: What coverage did the two-pool assay achieve for the lowest-load clinical specimen?
  • A: Only 58% at 100X for 8 copies, regardless of higher loads
  • B: About 95% of the genome at 100X for 8 copies, versus 98–99% for 22–675,885 copies
  • C: About 95% at 5,000X for 8 copies, with no coverage at higher loads
  • D: 100% at 100X for 8 copies and 95% for all higher loads

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The 13-specimen sequencing study reports high coverage at 100-fold depth even at the lowest measured viral load.

While about 95% of the genome was covered at 100X for 8 copies of virus, 98-99% of the genomes were covered at 100X for 22 to 675,885 copies of virus at an average sequencing depth of 5,000 reads per amplicon
Q7: What sequencing-depth trade-off was observed when subsampling clinical libraries?
  • A: All three depths yielded identical genome coverage above 99%
  • B: At 50,000 reads, 93% reached 100X; at 150,000 reads, only 58%
  • C: At 150,000 reads, at least 93% reached 100X; at 100,000 reads, 86%; and at 50,000 reads, at least 58%
  • D: Only 1 million reads per sample yielded any 100X coverage

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The coverage falls with shallower sequencing but remains substantial at tens to hundreds of thousands of total reads.

At least 93% of the genome was covered at 100X with 150,000 total reads, and 86% of the genome was covered at 100X with 100,000 total reads. Even at 50,000 total reads per library, at least 58% of the genome was covered at 100X
Q8: How did the authors reduce the risk of reporting false SARS-CoV-2 mutations in the clinical genomes?
  • A: They accepted mutations only if present in every specimen
  • B: They called all variants above 1% frequency using one algorithm
  • C: They used electrophoresis peak sizes to call all nucleotide substitutions
  • D: They required agreement of two independent algorithms and treated only variants above 60% allele frequency as empirically true

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

Cross-algorithm agreement and a high allele-frequency threshold were used for high-confidence mutation reporting.

The mutations in the SARS-CoV-2 genome from the 13 specimens were detected by two independently developed algorithms. Only those mutations that were detected by both methods were reported. Assuming all viral particles from a single patient contained identical mutations, mutations with frequencies (%AF) > 60% were considered to be empirically true.
Q9: What caveat did the authors attach to low-frequency intra-host mutation calls?
  • A: Without UMIs, low viral input and shallow reads can create PCR/sequencing noise and false calls; variants below 20% may also be missed
  • B: Low-frequency calls are unaffected by viral copy number or read depth
  • C: Every variant below 60% is guaranteed to be true intra-host diversity
  • D: The 20% threshold was selected because it captures all mutations exactly

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The authors warn that low input and read depth can depress allele frequency and introduce noise, while the ≥20% cutoff sacrifices some true rare variants.

Some true intra-host mutations with %AF < 20% might be missed. Since no UMI was used in the multiplex PCR amplification, intra-host mutations might still be contaminated with noise originating from PCR amplification, even though a 20% cutoff was applied. The occurrence of such noise may be exacerbated by low viral copy inputs in the multiplex PCR, or low read depth per amplicon in sequencing.
Q10: What feature made the metagenomic multiplex method more practical than untargeted metagenomic sequencing in the human-DNA background?
  • A: It sequenced only SARS-CoV-2-specific amplicons and could not detect unknown pathogens
  • B: Random hexamer-adapters plus background removal yielded 80% SARS-CoV-2 and 78% mitochondrial target coverage using about 0.6 million reads
  • C: It depleted all human DNA chemically and required more than 100 million reads
  • D: It achieved 13% viral read recovery only after host depletion

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The method enriched viral and mitochondrial regions without additional host depletion, requiring moderate sequencing depth.

In fact, our results showed that 16% of the recovered bases, or 13% of the recovered reads, were within the viral N and S genes (Fig. 4A and Supplemental Table 4 ). 80% of SARS-CoV-2 and 78% of mitochondrial targets were covered (Fig. 4B ), and their base coverage was significantly higher than for human targets (Fig. 4C ). In contrast, only 0.08% of human chromosomal regions were amplified.

Translational accuracy of a tethered ribosome

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: How did tethering affect programmed −1 frameshifting at the IS911, IS3, and dnaX sites when wild-type SD sequences were used?
  • A: Ribo-T reduced each event by approximately half relative to wild type
  • B: Ribo-T abolished all three events because it could not recognize wild-type SD sequences
  • C: Ribo-T retained similar efficiencies: 14% vs 13% for IS911, 33% for IS3 with either ribosome, and 45% vs 43% for dnaX
  • D: Ribo-T selectively enhanced IS3 to 60%, while IS911 and dnaX fell below 5%

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Despite the orthogonal anti-SD sequence, Ribo-T maintained near-wild-type −1 frameshifting on the tested signals, unlike its impaired +1 frameshifting.

the efficiency of -1 ribosomal frameshifting was 13% for IS911 with wild-type ribosomes and 14% with Ribo-T ribosomes (p-value of 0.54), 33% for IS3 with either type of ribosome (p-value of 0.77), 43% for dnaX with wild-type ribosomes and 45% with Ribo-T ribosomes
Q2: What was the central +1 frameshifting result for Ribo-T on the E. coli prfB signal, and did replacing the SD sequence rescue it?
  • A: Ribo-T achieved 14%, and orthogonal SD replacement restored wild type to 44%
  • B: Both ribosomes achieved about 30%, and SD replacement increased Ribo-T to 45%
  • C: Ribo-T achieved 33% versus 2% for wild type, and the orthogonal SD reduced it to 0.6%
  • D: Ribo-T achieved only 2% versus 33% for wild type, and the Ribo-T-recognized SD did not restore activity (1.8%)

Gold: D · No context: D · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The tethered ribosome is strongly impaired for +1 frameshifting, and changing to an SD it can recognize did not rescue the phenotype.

However, Ribo-T ribosomes frameshift inefficiently on this sequence, with an efficiency of only 2% (p-value of 8.9x10 -9 versus wild-type ribosomes
Q3: How did the investigators calculate translational efficiency in their dual-reporter assay?
  • A: They normalized the target luciferase/β-galactosidase ratio to the corresponding in-frame construct and used medians from at least six independent experiments
  • B: They divided β-galactosidase activity by optical density and averaged three technical replicates
  • C: They divided the target protein yield by mRNA abundance and normalized to cell growth rate
  • D: They measured luciferase alone relative to a no-SD background in one experiment per construct

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The assay uses a dual-reporter normalization against the corresponding in-frame construct and reports median values from at least six independent experiments.

The translational efficiency of the ribosomes was calculated as the ratio of luciferase activity to β-galactosidase activity for the target, divided by the ratio of luciferase activity to βgalactosidase activity for the corresponding in-frame construct.
Q4: What did the authors infer from the fourfold luciferase induction with the Ribo-T-specific SD sequence in the initiation reporter?
  • A: Ribo-T initiation was entirely SD-independent and the fourfold difference was caused by β-galactosidase
  • B: Ribo-T could use the orthogonal SD sequence, but less efficiently than wild-type ribosomes use the wild-type SD
  • C: Wild-type ribosomes preferred the orthogonal SD over their canonical SD
  • D: The orthogonal SD restored Ribo-T initiation to the wild-type level

Gold: B · No context: C · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

The Ribo-T-specific SD induced luciferase in the presence of Ribo-T, but this initiation was inefficient compared with wild-type ribosomes at a canonical SD.

Interestingly, in the presence of Ribo-T ribosomes, luciferase induction was increased by a factor of four (p-value 4.7x10 -4 ), indicating that Ribo-T ribosomes can use this alternative SD sequence, albeit inefficiently relative to WT ribosomes in the presence of a wild-type SD sequence
Q5: What experiment argued against scanning as the route by which ribosomes initiated the downstream luc gene in the lacZ-luc reporter?
  • A: Deleting the luc start codon did not affect β-galactosidase production
  • B: Changing the upstream SD sequence abolished all luciferase activity
  • C: A stable stem-loop placed after lacZ did not affect luc initiation (p=0.22), supporting internal loading
  • D: A stop codon was inserted before lacZ and increased luc initiation fourfold

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

A stable RNA structure would block scanning; its lack of effect on luc expression supports internal loading rather than scanning-mediated reinitiation.

We found that structure had no effect on the initiation of luc translation (p-value 0.22), strongly suggesting that the initiation of luc translation occurred through the internal loading of Ribo-T ribosomes
Q6: How did Ribo-T perform in stop-codon recognition relative to wild-type ribosomes?
  • A: Ribo-T differed only at UGA, while UAG and UAA were indistinguishable
  • B: Neither ribosome showed measurable readthrough for any tested stop codon

Gold: D · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The reported readthrough fractions were higher for Ribo-T than wild type for each stop codon, leading the authors to conclude termination is mildly impaired.

We found that Ribo-T ribosomes terminated translation less efficiently than wild-type ribosomes
Q7: Which reporter design enabled the authors to distinguish initiation by wild-type from Ribo-T ribosomes?
  • A: They paired the wild-type SD upstream of lacZ with a mutated CACCAC SD complementary to Ribo-T's altered anti-SD for Ribo-T assays
  • B: They used a common wild-type SD for both ribosomes and separated them by antibiotic resistance

  • D: They deleted both SD sequences and relied on different promoter strengths

Gold: A · No context: A · Dataset paper text: A · Existing Gemini summary: A · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: A

The altered SD sequence recruits Ribo-T selectively; in cells containing both ribosome types, only Ribo-T efficiently initiates at the orthogonal message.

For assessments of the translational efficiency of Ribo-T ribosomes, we used the corresponding constructs with a mutated SD sequence CACCAC upstream from the lacZ gene, in the presence of the vector encoding the Ribo-T ribosomes.
Q8: What growth and protein-synthesis costs were associated with Ribo-T in the prior characterization cited by the authors?
  • A: Doubling time was 50 min rather than 35 min, with equal protein-synthesis rates
  • B: Doubling time was 107 min rather than 35 min for wild type, and protein synthesis was 50% of wild-type rate
  • C: Doubling time was 35 min rather than 107 min, and protein synthesis doubled
  • D: Doubling time was unchanged, but protein synthesis fell to 2% of wild type

Gold: B · No context: B · Dataset paper text: B · Existing Gemini summary: B · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: B

Tethered ribosomes support growth but have a slower-growth phenotype and reduced overall protein synthesis, attributed largely to slower biogenesis.

The resulting ribosome, Ribo-T, with its inseparable subunits, was shown to be functional and able to sustain the cell growth, although doubling times were slower (107 min for Ribo-T, 35 min for wild-type ribosomes).
Q9: What structural explanation did the authors propose for the broad inefficiency of SD use by Ribo-T?
  • A: The orthogonal SD sequence is methylated and therefore cannot pair with any ribosome
  • B: The 23S rRNA binding pocket blocks the mRNA start codon from entering the A site
  • C: Tethering through helix 44 may alter the orientation of the anti-SD sequence, impairing its normal interactions
  • D: The tether deletes the 16S rRNA anti-SD sequence completely

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

Because the tether links subunits through H44, the authors suggest altered H44 geometry could reorient the anti-SD and explain reduced SD use during initiation and elongation.

We therefore suggest that its orientation is modified in Ribo-T ribosomes, preventing them from using SD sequences in a normal manner during translation initiation and elongation.
Q10: Which limitation did the authors identify as necessary to resolve the proposed H44-based explanation?
  • A: The assay must be repeated in yeast because bacterial ribosomes lack H44
  • B: The mutant ribosome must be purified from all cells before any reporter interpretation is possible

Efficient and Flexible Integration of Variant Characteristics in Rare Variant Association Studies Using Integrated Nested Laplace Approximation

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: What distinction between BATI and MiST motivated the authors' use of INLA?

  • C: BATI removes variant characteristics, whereas MiST integrates them as fixed effects

The paper frames BATI as an INLA-based Bayesian alternative enabling full model estimation without the expensive integration required by MiST's model.

with the motivation of making inference based on full model estimation, we propose a Bayesian alternative to the GLMM, using the Integrated Nested Laplace Approximation (INLA) for efficient model estimation
Q2: Which issue with conventional MCMC inference did the authors emphasize in motivating INLA for genome-wide scans?

The introduction cites difficult convergence evaluation and expensive computation, particularly problematic for genome-wide analyses.

𝐻 0 : 𝜂 𝑖 = 𝑋 𝑡 𝑖 𝛼 (6) 𝐻 1 : 𝜂 𝑖 = 𝑋 𝑡 𝑖 𝛼 + (𝐺 𝑡 𝑖 𝑍)𝜔 + 𝐺 𝑡 𝑖 𝛿

A Combined Evidence Approach to Prioritize Nipah Virus Inhibitors

Dataset text · Existing summary · KUs

Q1: How did the authors use abstract-level relevance scores when screening the literature for the Nipah inhibitor knowledgebase?
  • A: Only papers scoring 10 were retained, while peptide inhibitors scored 0
  • B: Papers were scored by IC50, with 5 nM as the inclusion cutoff
  • C: Papers with confirmed inhibitor information scored 10, uncertain relevance 5, peptide inhibitors 4, and unrelated papers 0; scores 10, 5, and 4 proceeded
  • D: All 838 papers were curated without relevance scoring

Gold: C · No context: C · Dataset paper text: C · Existing Gemini summary: C · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: C

The curation pipeline began with a keyword search, then manually curated the relevance-scored subset.

At the time of curation this keyword listed 838 papers which were considered for data-mining. This step was followed by a scoring system wherein papers that had confirmed information regarding the inhibitors against Nipah virus in their abstracts were assigned a score of '10', those that may or may not have information regarding the same were given a score of '5', those with information on peptides used as inhibitors were assigned a score of '4' and rest with no relevant information were given a score of '0'. Papers with score values of 10, 5 and 4 were considered for further study
Q2: What distinction did the authors make between total knowledgebase entries and the small-molecule set used for most prioritization?
  • A: NVIK had 182 unique small molecules and 125 non-small-molecule records
  • B: NVIK had 98 entries total, of which 70 were peptides
  • C: NVIK had 125 entries, all unique small molecules, and 182 assay values
  • D: NVIK had 182 entries; 125 were focus entries, of which 98 were unique small-molecule inhibitors

Gold: D · No context: A · Dataset paper text: D · Existing Gemini summary: D · Qwen27B Knowledge Units: D

The entry count includes peptides and incomplete records; the focused small-molecule subset was smaller and included multiple assays for some compounds.

At present, NVIK has 182 entries. The focus of the study was to evaluate small molecule inhibitors which were 125 entries as the rest 57 were either peptide or did not have complete information. Of these 125 entries, 98 are unique nipah inhibitors as for a few compounds more than one assay has been curated from the literature.

Of these, EC50/IC50 values could be obtained for 70 compounds and structures are available for all 98 inhibitors. The EC50/IC50 values range from 0.25 nM to 7562710 nM for 182 entries and 0.47 nM to 7562710 nM for 125 small molecule entries.

Different tumors, specifically syngeneic lines, should be similarly studied to test the generalizability of the novel phenomenon observed in the current study
